1	# Assignment 1 — Evaluation and flexibility
2	
3	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
4	Released Thursday, September 3 · **Due Thursday, October 1, 6:00 PM Central** · 10% of course grade
5	
6	Covers Handouts 1–3: supervised learning and loss, training versus test error, cross-validation, choosing a split that matches deployment, logistic regression, model flexibility, bias and variance, and regularization.
7	
8	**Expected effort: 6–8 focused hours.** Part A is the larger of the two parts.
9	
10	Two independent cases, one memo.
11	
12	- **Part A — Northline Fitness.** Build an evaluation for a churn model that reflects how the model would actually be used, and turn its predictions into a targeting decision priced in dollars. Uses Handouts 1–2.
13	- **Part B — Cascade Outdoor.** Measure how much of the forecast error in two retail divisions is unavoidable, compare four models against that floor, and say what a company-wide modelling standard should specify. Uses Handout 3.
14	
15	Part A can be started the day the assignment is released. Part B needs Handout 3, covered in Lecture 4; you will have at least two weeks after that. Midterm 1 on September 24 covers Lectures 1–4, so this assignment is direct preparation for it, and the deadline falls a week after the exam.
16	
17	---
18	
19	# Part A — Northline Fitness
20	
21	## The situation
22	
23	Northline Fitness sells a subscription fitness app. Members pay monthly on one of three plans. About 9% of active members cancel in any 60-day window.
24	
25	The retention team wants to run a monthly program: score every active member for the probability they cancel within 60 days, and send the highest-scoring members a **retention offer** — one month at 50% off plus a personal check-in.
26	
27	Finance has priced it:
28	
29	| Quantity | Value |
30	|---|---|
31	| Cost of extending one offer | **$18** |
32	| Lifetime value of a member who stays | **$240** |
33	| Fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer | **0.35** |
34	| Effect on a member who was not going to churn | none; the $18 is still spent |
35	
36	So an offer to a member who would have churned is worth `240 × 0.35 - 18 = $66`. An offer to a member who would not have churned costs `$18`. No offer is worth `$0`.
37	
38	A junior analyst has already pulled 24 months of history and fitted a first model: logistic regression on all available columns, 5-fold cross-validation, encoders and scalers fitted inside the folds. It reports **0.967 AUC**. The code is in `part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py` and runs as-is.
39	
40	The VP of Membership wants a second opinion before this goes to the operating committee. **Your job is to produce the estimate she can plan against, and a recommendation.**
41	
42	## How the model would be deployed
43	
44	If approved, the program runs monthly from **April 2026**, scoring the active book at the start of each month. Two things about that book differ from the history you have:
45	
46	1. **Corporate members.** Northline signed three employer wellness contracts starting February 2025. The `corporate` channel is 0% of the book in 2024, about 20% by December 2025, and Membership Analytics forecasts **roughly 35% of the April–June 2026 book**.
47	2. **A competitor** launched a rival app in July 2025 and is still spending heavily.
48	
49	## What you need to know about the data
50	
51	All of this is also in `part1_northline/data/DATA_DICTIONARY.md`. None of it is hidden; the work is in handling it correctly, not in finding it.
52	
53	- **Grain.** One row per member per calendar month. A member appears in many rows. The 4,200 members produce 33,340 rows.
54	- **Timing.** The dictionary states, for every column, the window over which it is measured. Some columns are recorded **after** the month being scored, and so would not exist at the moment the April 2026 model has to produce a score. Use the timing notes to decide which columns are admissible; keeping a column is a decision you should be able to justify just as much as dropping one.
55	- **Duplicated rows (ticket NLF-2231).** The March 2025 load was re-run after a partition failure and appended instead of replacing. Every affected row appears twice, identical on every column. Remove the duplicates before modelling.
56	- **Date formats (ticket NLF-2198).** `snapshot_month` appears in three formats. `load.py` provides `month_key()` to normalise them.
57	
58	## A1 — Baselines (before any modelling)
59	
60	Report, in one small table:
61	
62	1. The base rate of `churn_next_60d`.
63	2. The accuracy of predicting "no churn" for everyone, and one sentence on whether accuracy is the right metric for this decision.
64	3. AUC for one single-variable rule — for example, ranking members by `days_since_last_login`.
65	4. The analyst's headline AUC (0.967) beside them.
66	
67	Then, in one short paragraph: what would have to be true about this dataset for 0.967 to be a good estimate of performance on the April 2026 book?
68	
69	## A2 — Build an evaluation that matches deployment
70	
71	Make the following four changes to the analyst's protocol, **one at a time, in an order you choose**, and record the AUC after each. Report it as a table.
72	
73	Until you have made change 3 the number is cross-validated on the building data; from change 3 onward it is measured on the test window defined below. Say in the table which kind each row is.
74	
75	| Change | What it means |
76	|---|---|
77	| **1. Admissible columns only** | Keep only columns that exist at the moment of scoring, per the timing notes in the dictionary. |
78	| **2. One member, not many rows** | A member contributes many rows. Make sure a member never appears in both the fitting data and the held-out data. |
79	| **3. Past to future** | Fit on earlier months and evaluate on later ones, rather than on a random shuffle of all months. |
80	| **4. Report by segment** | Report the final number separately for `corporate` members and for everyone else. |
81	
82	**One of these four changes will move the number a great deal, and at least one will move it barely at all.** That is expected. A change can be the right thing to do and still be worth almost nothing on a particular dataset, and reporting a near-zero effect correctly earns the same marks as reporting a large one. Do not go looking for a bug because a number did not move.
83	
84	For each row of the table, write one sentence on what the number means. Then, in two or three sentences:
85	
86	- Why you chose that order.
87	- Whether the changes between rows can be read as "problem X cost this much AUC." (They cannot be read that way independently of the order; say why, in your own words.)
88	
89	**Use these evaluation windows** for the final protocol, so that everyone's numbers are comparable:
90	
91	| Window | Months | Purpose |
92	|---|---|---|
93	| Training | 2024-01 → 2025-06 | Fit the model |
94	| Not used | 2025-07, 2025-08 | Labels for June 2025 do not mature until end of August 2025 |
95	| Validation | 2025-09 | Any choice made by looking at data |
96	| Test | 2025-10 → 2025-12 | Final estimate; score once |
97	
98	Explain in one sentence why the two-month gap is there.
99	
100	## A3 — The targeting decision
101	
102	Using the final protocol from A2:
103	
104	1. Report test-period AUC overall, for corporate members, and for everyone else, each with row counts and churner counts.
105	2. **Derive the cost-based threshold** from the finance figures in the table above. Write down what an offer is worth to a member who would have churned and to one who would not, take the expectation at predicted probability `p`, and find the value of `p` above which making the offer is worth more than not making it. Show the working and state the threshold. Handout R1 §3 covers the general form; derive it here rather than quoting it, since you will be asked to on the midterm.
106	3. Report the realised dollar value of the program on the test period at that threshold, and compare it with offering to every member.
107	4. **State your recommendation** to the VP. If it is conditional, state the condition so that someone at Northline could check whether it holds — for example, in terms of a share of the book or a measured AUC.
108	
109	---
110	
111	# Part B — Cascade Outdoor
112	
113	## The situation
114	
115	Cascade Outdoor is a specialty retailer with two merchandising divisions: **Hardgoods** (tents, packs, stoves) and **Apparel** (jackets, base layers). Both forecast weekly units per store to drive buying and markdown decisions. Both run on the same planning system and record the same twelve fields.
116	
117	They differ in size. Apparel migrated to the new platform in March and its pre-migration history is not comparable, so it was dropped. **Hardgoods has about 3,000 usable store-weeks; Apparel has 197.**
118	
119	A contractor fitted ordinary least squares to each division twice — once on the twelve fields (degree 1, twelve terms) and once on a degree-2 polynomial basis (every square and pairwise product, ninety terms) — and scored both by 5-fold cross-validated MSE:
120	
121	| | degree 1 | degree 2 |
122	|---|---:|---:|
123	| Hardgoods | 336 | **231** |
124	| Apparel | **414** | 618 |
125	
126	He drew two conclusions: that the divisions need **different models**, since a different specification wins in each; and that **Apparel demand is inherently harder to forecast**, since its best error of 414 is nearly double Hardgoods' 231.
127	
128	The VP of Merchandising Planning wants **one modelling standard** company-wide, and has asked you whether that is possible and what it should say.
129	
130	## What Part B asks
131	
132	1. How much of the error in each division is unavoidable?
133	2. What does a penalty buy, and why is the answer different in a division with 3,000 rows than in one with 197?
134	3. Should Cascade standardise a model, or a process?
135	
136	There are no marks for a better model than the four specified below.
137	
138	## What you need to know about the data
139	
140	Also in `part2_cascade/data/DATA_DICTIONARY.md`.
141	
142	- **`replicates.csv`** is a controlled exercise: 45 store-week configurations per division, each observed **ten times** with identical values on all twelve recorded fields. None of these rows appear in the training extracts. You use it for two things: estimating the noise floor, and scoring your final models once.
143	- **Duplicated rows (ticket CO-4471).** The Hardgoods load was re-run and appended rather than replaced, so affected store-weeks appear twice. `dedup_h()` in `load.py` removes them. Use it for everything in Part B.
144	- **Apparel cleaning.** Three Apparel weeks were removed before delivery (a store closure, a keying error, and Black Friday). The dictionary records the reasoning; you do not need to revisit it.
145	
146	## The protocol — use exactly this
147	
148	`part2_cascade/starter/load.py` provides all of it.
149	
150	| Step | What to do |
151	|---|---|
152	| **Tune** | 5-fold CV on the division's own training extract, using `make_folds()`. Fit preprocessing inside each fold — `poly_pipeline(estimator, degree=d)` does this. |
153	| **Refit** | Refit each tuned configuration on all rows of that division's training extract. |
154	| **Test** | Score once on `test_extract("H")` or `test_extract("A")`. Nothing is selected by looking at these rows. |
155	| **Report** | Test MSE, and **test MSE − σ̂²** — the part of the error a better model could in principle remove. |
156	
157	Standardising before penalising is not optional; `poly_pipeline()` handles it. Handout 3 §5.2 explains why.
158	
159	## B1 — The noise floor
160	
161	Each group of ten rows in `replicates.csv` is identical on every recorded field, so the variation within a group is what no model could have predicted. Estimate σ² for each division as the pooled within-configuration variance:
162	
163	$$\hat{\sigma}^2 = \frac{\sum_g \sum_r (y_{gr} - \bar{y}_g)^2}{\sum_g (m_g - 1)}$$
164	
165	Report both estimates with their approximate standard error, $\hat{\sigma}^2\sqrt{2/\text{dof}}$, where the degrees of freedom are the denominator of $\hat{\sigma}^2$ above:
166	
167	$$\text{dof} \;=\; \sum_g (m_g - 1),$$
168	
169	that is, one fewer than the group size, summed over groups. With 45 configurations of 10 replicates in a division, $\text{dof} = 45 \times 9 = 405$.
170	
171	Then, in one or two sentences: what do the two floors say about the contractor's claim that Apparel demand is inherently harder to forecast?
172	
173	## B2 — Four models, compared against the floor
174	
175	Two degrees crossed with two treatments, in both divisions:
176	
177	| Model | How to fit it | Tuning |
178	|---|---|---|
179	| Degree-1 OLS | `poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=1)` | none |
180	| Degree-1 ridge | `poly_pipeline(Ridge(), degree=1)` | `ALPHA_GRID`, 5-fold CV |
181	| Degree-2 OLS | `poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=2)` | none |
182	| Degree-2 ridge | `poly_pipeline(Ridge(), degree=2)` | `ALPHA_GRID`, 5-fold CV |
183	
184	For each of the eight cells report: the tuned penalty where there is one, CV MSE with its standard error across folds, test MSE, and test MSE − σ̂².
185	
186	Then answer, in two or three sentences each, citing numbers from your own table:
187	
188	1. **Where does the penalty change the answer, and where does it not?** Compare the CV MSE with and without a penalty in all four cells. Say what distinguishes the cell where it matters most. (Note that degree-1 Apparel is also a small sample, so "Apparel is small" is not the full explanation — the useful quantity is the number of terms relative to the number of rows.)
189	2. **Is a single company-wide specification possible?** The contractor compared unpenalised fits only. Once each specification is allowed a tuned penalty, is there one choice that is at least as good as the best hand-picked choice in *both* divisions? Note where the one-standard-error rule (Handout 1 §6.5) bears on your answer.
190	3. **How firm is the Apparel ordering?** Put the Apparel CV standard errors next to the Hardgoods ones and say which differences in Apparel are large relative to their uncertainty and which are not.
191	
192	## B3 — One stability experiment
193	
194	`part2_cascade/starter/stability.py` contains the loop; fill in the four models and interpret the output. Everything else is fixed: **20 bootstrap resamples** from `bootstrap_indices()`, **three query configurations** per division from `QUERY_CONFIGS`, and penalties **held at the B2 values** (do not re-tune inside the resamples).
195	
196	Each query configuration also appears in `replicates.csv`, so the mean of its ten replicates estimates the true mean demand there to within about σ/√10 ≈ 4 units. That is what lets you see bias and variance separately rather than only their sum.
197	
198	For each division and model, report at each query point:
199	
200	- the **standard deviation** of the 20 predictions (variance), and
201	- the **difference between their mean and the replicate-based estimate of the truth** (bias).
202	
203	Report the average of each across the three points, plus one figure.
204	
205	Then, in one sentence each, backed by a number from your table:
206	
207	1. What raising the degree from 1 to 2 did to bias and to variance, in each division.
208	2. What the penalty did to each, at each degree.
209	3. Why the same four procedures give different answers in the two divisions. Name the quantity that differs and connect it to B2.
210	
211	If one of these effects is nearly zero, say so and give the number.
212	
213	**Optional, no marks, about two minutes.** `part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py` plots training and cross-validated error against the number of terms in the basis, and cross-validated error against the ridge penalty, for both divisions. Running it is the fastest way to see the curves in Handout 3 §1.4, and Midterm 1 will ask you to read a plot like them.
214

Answer the three Part B questions, and say what a company-wide standard should fix and what it should leave to the data, in terms someone could apply to a third division acquired next year.
224

254	| Path | What it is |
255	|---|---|
256	| `assignments/00_start_here/01_how_to_approach_assignments.md` | Read before you begin. Short. |
257	| `assignments/00_start_here/02_ai_coding_agent_policy.md` | What you may delegate, and which agents are approved |
258	| `assignments/00_start_here/03_exporting_your_session_history.md` | How to produce `sessions/` |
259	| `assignments/00_start_here/04_submission_checklist.md` | Submission format |
260	| `handouts/lecture01_learning_from_data.pdf` … `lecture03_bias_variance.pdf` | Handouts 1–3 |
261	| `handouts/handoutR1_reading_a_metric.pdf` | Handout R1 — every metric used here, with worked examples |
262	
263	## Where the concepts are
264	
265	| For | Read |
266	|---|---|
267	| Loss as a business statement | Handout 1, §4.4 |
268	| Risk, empirical risk, why training error is not evidence | Handout 1, §5 |
269	| Cross-validation, what it estimates, its standard error | Handout 1, §6 |
270	| The one-standard-error rule | Handout 1, §6.5 |
271	| Leakage and the test that catches it | Handout 1, §7.2 |
272	| Splits that match deployment; grouped and temporal folds; reporting by segment | Handout 1, §7.3–7.4 |
273	| Logistic regression; reading a coefficient as an odds ratio | Handout 2, §7.2 |
274	| The flexibility dial; why the cross-validated minimum is interior | Handout 3, §1 and §4 |
275	| Irreducible error as a floor | Handout 3, §3.3 |
276	| The bias–variance decomposition | Handout 3, §3 |
277	| Ridge; what a penalty buys and costs; why you standardise first | Handout 3, §5.2 |
278	
279	**Metrics — accuracy, precision, recall, ROC, AUC, R², RMSE, calibration — are in Handout R1, *Reading a Metric*.** Look a metric up when you need it rather than reading it front to back. The parts this assignment uses:
280	
281	| For | Read |
282	|---|---|
283	| Accuracy, and why a 9% base rate makes it a poor headline | Handout R1, §4.2 |
284	| Confusion matrix; precision and recall | Handout R1, §4.1, §4.3 |
285	| ROC and AUC | Handout R1, §4.5 |
286	| Turning a probability into a send / don't-send decision from costs | Handout R1, §3 — the general form; A3 asks you to derive it |
287	| R², RMSE, MAE; error measured against a floor | Handout R1, §2 |
288	
289	## Ground rules
290	
291	- **Score each test set once.** Northline's test period and Cascade's replicate extract are used at the end and select nothing.
292	- **State every Part B error against the floor.** A raw MSE has no interpretation on its own.
293	- **Every number in the memo must be reproducible** from your submitted code, with one command.
294	
295	## Two practical habits
296	
297	**Do not let your agent read a CSV into the conversation.** `member_months.csv` is 33,000 rows; pasting it can cost more in one message than a whole working session should. Have the agent write code that reads the file and reports numbers back. The data dictionaries are short and are meant to be read.
298	
299	**Start a fresh session for each work block** — the audit table, then the threshold, then Part B — rather than one session running all month. It keeps costs down and makes your transcripts easier to export and read. `../00_start_here/05_setting_up_your_agent.md` has the rest.
300

1	# Assignment 1 — grading rubric
2	
3	100 points, scaled to 10% of the course grade.
4	
5	Part A carries 55 points, Part B 35, and submission quality 10.
6	
7	## Part A — Northline Fitness
8	
9	| # | Criterion | Pts |
10	|---|---|---:|
11	| A1 | **Baselines.** Base rate, accuracy of the trivial rule with a correct statement of whether accuracy suits this decision, at least one single-variable AUC, and the analyst's headline beside them. The paragraph on what would have to be true for 0.967 to be credible. | **8** |
12	| A2 | **The evaluation.** All four changes made one at a time, AUC reported after each, the ordering justified, each row interpreted in a sentence, and each row labelled as cross-validated or test-window. Column decisions argued from availability at the moment of scoring, for columns kept as well as dropped. Correct statement of why the differences between rows are not an order-independent decomposition. Correct explanation of the two-month gap. | **27** |
13	| A3 | **The decision.** Test AUC overall and by segment with row and churner counts; the cost-based threshold derived rather than quoted; realised program value at that threshold against offering to everyone; a recommendation whose condition, if any, is stated so it can be checked. | **20** |
14	
15	## Part B — Cascade Outdoor
16	
17	| # | Criterion | Pts |
18	|---|---|---:|
19	| B1 | **The noise floor.** σ̂² for both divisions with its uncertainty, computed correctly as pooled within-configuration variance, and a correct statement of what the two floors imply about the "Apparel is inherently harder" claim. | **8** |
20	| B2 | **Comparison against the floor.** All eight cells tuned and reported under the prescribed protocol, with results stated as distance from σ̂². The penalty's effect quantified in all four cells and attributed to the ratio of terms to rows. The single-standard question answered against penalised fits, with the one-standard-error rule used. CV standard errors reported and used to say which Apparel differences are supported. | **19** |
21	| B3 | **Stability.** Bias and variance reported for all four models in both divisions, plus the figure. Three interpretive claims, each tied to a number in the student's own table rather than asserted from the lecture. | **8** |
22	
23	## Submission
24	
25	| # | Criterion | Pts |
26	|---|---|---:|
27	| S | **Reproducibility, communication and process.** Runs end to end from one command on a clean copy of the folder. Every number in the memo comes from that run. Both memo sections within length and addressed to their named reader, each leading with a recommendation. Session transcripts present for every session used. AI process note specific about what was delegated, what was changed or rejected, and how numbers were checked. | **10** |
28	
29	A2 and B2 are 46 of the 100 points. That is deliberate: the assignment is about what an evaluation matched to deployment does to a headline number, and about what changes between a division with 3,000 rows and one with 197.
30	
31	---
32	
33	## Common ways marks are lost
34	
35	- **Making several changes at once in A2.** A row that removes columns, regroups by member and re-splits by time attributes the change to nothing in particular.
36	- **Reading the A2 table as a decomposition.** The differences between rows depend on the order you made the changes in.
37	- **Dropping columns without a timing argument.** Both keeping and dropping a column need to be justified from when the column is measured.
38	- **Selecting anything on a test set.** Northline's test period and Cascade's replicate extract are each scored once.
39	- **A raw MSE with no floor beside it.** B2 asks for distance from σ̂².
40	- **Generalising one division to both.** The answer differs between Hardgoods and Apparel; that difference is the content of Part B.
41	- **Reporting an ordering without its uncertainty.** In Apparel some differences are inside the noise. Saying so scores better than declaring a winner the data cannot support.
42	- **Lecture vocabulary in place of your own numbers in B3.** "Ridge reduces variance" is a sentence from the handout; B3 asks for the number from your table.
43	- **A "standard" that is really a model.** The VP asked for something portable to a division acquired next year. Fixing a polynomial degree in advance has not answered her.
44	- **A generic AI process note.** "Used AI to help write code, verified output" earns nothing.
45

[tool_result]
1	# How to approach the assignments
2	
3	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
4	Read this once before Assignment 1, and re-read §5 before each submission.
5	
6	---
7	
8	## 1. What is being graded
9	
10	You have a coding agent (`05_setting_up_your_agent.md`). It can write a modeling pipeline faster
11	than you can describe one, so writing the pipeline is not the skill being assessed.
12	
13	What is assessed is the part that stays yours:
14	
15	- deciding what question is actually being asked
16	- noticing when an answer is too good
17	- knowing what evidence would settle a disagreement
18	- saying what the evidence does and does not support
19	
20	The broad goal of these assignments is to help you become comfortable working with AI to define and implement machine learning pipelines in a business context. To do that, you need a certain level of competency in the core concepts of machine learning. And imparting that competency is the broad goal of the course. 
21	
22	Each assignment is preparation for an in-person exam where you will have none of these tools. If you
23	finish an assignment and could not explain your headline number on paper, it has not done its job.
24	
25	---
26	
27	## 2. What every assignment looks like
28	
29	A business situation, a dataset, and **an analysis someone else has already done** — an analyst, a
30	contractor, an engineer. That analysis is plausible, arithmetically correct, but also wrong. Your task is
31	not "build a model." It is "understand what is wrong, why it is wrong, establish what is true, and say what should be done."
32	
33	Four things are submitted every time:
34	
35	| | |
36	|---|---|
37	| `memo.pdf` or `memo.md` | To a named business reader. Length limit stated per assignment, and enforced. |
38	| `analysis/` | Your code. Runs end to end from one command. |
39	| `ai_process_note.md` | One page. What you delegated, what you rejected, how you checked. |
40	| `sessions/` | Complete exported history of every AI session used. |
41	
42	---
43	
44	## 3. A working order that tends to go well
45	
46	**Read the brief and the data dictionary yourself first.** The dictionaries record how each column
47	is produced and *when*. That timing information decides which columns you may use, and it is easier
48	to take in before a model exists to distract you.
49	
50	**Write down what you expect before you compute anything.** What is the base rate? What order of
51	magnitude should the effect be? What would a suspiciously good result look like? Two minutes of this
52	is what makes you able to be surprised, and being surprised is how you find things.
53	
54	**Get a baseline before you get a model.** Every assignment has a do-nothing answer — predict the
55	majority class, predict the mean, keep the current price. Compute it. It is the denominator for
56	everything that follows and the number most often missing from an agent's output.
57	
58	**Change one thing at a time.** Where an assignment asks for an audit trail — one row per problem,
59	one fix, and the resulting number — build it as you go rather than reconstructing it at the end. It
60	is also your best defence against an agent that quietly fixed three things at once.
61	
62	---
63	
64	## 4. Working with your agent
65	
66	The assignments are graded lightly, and in principle, you can delegate the majority of work to AI. However, this goes against the course's learning goals and will make you struggle in the in-person assessments. Hence, the following strategy is recommended for optimal learning.
67	
68	**Delegate the work, not the judgment.** Good to hand over: "write a grouped cross-validation split
69	by member_id", "sweep the ridge penalty over the grid and plot train and validation error". Not good
70	to hand over: "tell me whether this model is good enough to deploy", "write the recommendation."
71	The second list is the assignment.
72	
73	**Give it the business context, not just the file.** An agent told "predict churn from this CSV" will
74	optimise accuracy. An agent told what an offer costs and what a retained member is worth will do
75	something quite different. The context is in the brief; pass it along.
76	
77	**Ask it to argue against itself.** "What would have to be true for this estimate to be wrong?" and
78	"What is the strongest objection to this conclusion?" are the two highest-yield prompts in the course.
79	
80	**Be suspicious of agreement.** If the first answer confirms what you expected and nothing errored,
81	slow down rather than speed up.
82	
83	**Do not let it choose the evaluation design.** Splits, metrics, units of analysis, and what counts as
84	held out are the graded content of this course. An agent will pick a sensible default, and these
85	datasets are built to punish sensible defaults.
86	
87	**If you do not understand what it produced, stop.** Get it explained or throw it out. You are
88	responsible for every line and may be asked in class to explain any of it.
89	
90	**Export your session before you close it** (`03_exporting_your_session_history.md`). The Codex app
91	copies to the clipboard rather than to a file, so paste it somewhere immediately.
92	
93	---
94	
95	## 5. The memo
96	
97	Written to a named person who has a decision to make and will read the first paragraph carefully and
98	the rest quickly.
99	
100	- **Lead with the recommendation.** Not the method, not the data cleaning.
101	- **Give the number, then the number it replaces.** "The honest estimate is X, not the Y in the
102	  current proposal, and the gap is mostly Z."
103	- **Quantify the uncertainty once, plainly, and move on.** A memo that never commits is not useful.
104	- **State conditions so someone could check them.** "Deploy if corporate accounts stay under 15% of
105	  the book" is a recommendation. "Deploy with caution" is not.
106	- **Respect the length limit.** Exhibits do not count against prose length; prose does.
107	
108	---
109	
110	## 6. Before you submit
111	
112	Full list in `04_submission_checklist.md`. The five that matter most:
113	
114	- [ ] The analysis runs end to end, from a clean copy of the folder, with one command.
115	- [ ] Every number in the memo comes from that run.
116	- [ ] Every session is exported into `sessions/`, complete and unedited.
117	- [ ] The process note names specific things you rejected or corrected. If you rejected nothing all
118	      assignment, think about that before writing it down.
119	- [ ] You can explain any part of the submission out loud, without notes.
120	
121	That last item is not decorative. You may be asked to explain or modify a piece of your submitted
122	work in a short oral check. 
123

[tool_result]
1	# Policy on generative AI and coding agents
2	
3	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
4	
5	This policy governs the three take-home assignments. It supplements, and does not replace, the university's academic integrity policy.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## The short version
10	
11	**On assignments, use one of the two approved agents freely, and disclose completely.**
12	**In the exam room, use nothing.**
13	**You own everything you submit.**
14	
15	---
16	
17	## 1. Why the policy is written this way
18	
19	You will graduate into work where directing an AI system through an analysis is ordinary. Pretending otherwise in a graduate business course would be a disservice. So the assignments are AI-enabled by design, and they are built to help you become comfortable in working with AI in designing and implementing Machine Learning pipelines.
20	
21	The trade is that the assignments carry 30% of the grade — 10% each — and the in-person exams carry the other 70%. The assignments are where you learn; the exams are where independent understanding is evidenced. They are also built on the same material as the exam that follows each of them, so treating an assignment as something to get past rather than something to learn from costs twice: once at 10%, and again on the exam it was preparation for.
22	
23	---
24	
25	## 1a. The approved agents
26	
27	**This course uses one of two coding agents: the Codex app or Claude Code. No other coding agent may
28	be used on coursework.**
29	
30	Both produce a complete, exportable session history, which is the property the course requires and
31	is not universal among these tools. Standardizing on two means the setup instructions apply to
32	everyone, the transcripts are in formats I can read quickly, and help in class applies to the whole
33	room.
34	
35	**Codex has a free path** — the Free plan plus the $100 student credit grant OpenAI makes available
36	to verified students at US and Canadian universities — so nobody needs to spend money to complete an
37	assignment. Claude Code requires a Claude plan that includes it. Setup for both, and how to claim
38	the grant, is in `05_setting_up_your_agent.md`.
39	
40	
41	
42	## 2. Permitted
43	
44	- Using the Codex app or Claude Code on any part of a take-home assignment.
45	- Asking for explanations, code, debugging help, alternative approaches, critique of your reasoning, and critique of your writing.
46	- Using either approved agent, for many sessions across many days.
47	- Using AI to help you draft the memo, provided the judgments in it are yours and you can defend them.
48	- Discussing course concepts with classmates.
49	
50	There is no expectation that you minimise your AI use, and no credit for having used less of it.
51	
52	## 3. Required
53	
54	**Work inside the assignment folder.** Each assignment ships as a bounded folder. Keep your work there.
55	
56	**Export the complete session history for every AI session you use, and submit all of it.** Not a summary, not the interesting parts, not one representative session. If you opened five sessions, all five are submitted. `03_exporting_your_session_history.md` explains how, for each supported tool.
57	
58	**Validate outputs.** Every number in your memo must be reproducible from your submitted code. If your agent reported a figure you cannot regenerate, it does not go in the memo.
59	
60	**Document your corrections.** The AI process note asks what you delegated, what you changed or rejected, and how you checked the result. This is the part of the submission that most directly evidences your judgment, and it is graded on specificity.
61	
62	**Be able to explain and modify your work.** You may be asked, in class or in a short oral check, to walk through a piece of your submission or make a small change to it. This applies to every line you submit regardless of who or what wrote it.
63	
64	## 4. Not permitted
65	
66	- **Any generative AI, internet access, or communication device during the in-person midterms and final.** Permitted materials will be announced in advance.
67	- **Using any coding agent other than the Codex app or Claude Code** on assignments.
68	- **Concealing AI use, or using a tool whose sessions you do not disclose.**
69	- **Editing, truncating, or reconstructing a transcript** to change what it shows. Submitting a hand-written "transcript" of a session you did not save is fabrication, not a formatting shortcut.
70	- **Sharing or receiving code, memos, prompt logs, completed folders, or final answers.** The assignments are individual. Discussing concepts is encouraged; exchanging artifacts is not.
71	- **Submitting work you cannot explain.**
72	- **Entering passwords, API keys, or confidential personal or employer data into an AI tool.** All course data is synthetic and safe to share with a model. Your own material may not be.
73	
74	---
75	
76	## 5. What the transcripts are, and are not
77	
78	**They are an audit trail, not proof of understanding.** A long transcript does not earn marks and a short one does not lose them. The instructor may review logs selectively — typically when a submission's quality and its process note do not match each other, or when a memo contains a claim the submitted code does not produce.
79	
80	**They are not a second deliverable.** Do not curate them, annotate them, or write them for an audience. Export and attach.
81	
82	**They are read alongside your process note.** The most common signal of a problem is not a transcript showing heavy AI use; it is a transcript showing that a conclusion arrived fully formed and was never questioned, paired with a process note claiming careful validation.
83	
84	---
85	
86	## 6. If an export goes wrong
87	
88	Both approved agents produce a complete session history, so the usual cause of a gap is a mechanical
89	one: a clipboard copy that did not land, a session closed before it was saved, a transcript that
90	expired. That is recoverable if you say so and unrecoverable if you do not.
91	
92	If a session you used is missing or incomplete:
93	
94	1. Submit whatever you do have.
95	2. Add a short note in `sessions/README.md` stating which sessions are affected, roughly how many,
96	   what is missing, and what you used them for.
97	3. Export at the end of every working session from then on, so it does not happen twice.
98	
99	A disclosed gap is fine. An undisclosed one is a violation.
100	
101	---
102	
103	## 7. Privacy warning, read this once
104	
105	Session transcripts from Codex and Claude Code **embed the full content of files the agent read and the full output of commands it ran**. If you work on assignment material in a directory that also contains something private, that private material can end up inside the transcript you submit.
106	
107	Do the assignments in a clean folder. Before submitting, open your export and look at it. You are responsible for what is in it.
108	
109	---
110	
111	## 8. Consequences
112	
113	Concealed AI use, fabricated or edited transcripts, and shared artefacts are academic integrity violations and are referred through the university process. Because each assignment is worth 10%, the cost of a violation is enormously out of proportion to anything it could gain. There is no version of these assignments where dishonesty is the rational choice.
114	
115	If you are unsure whether something is permitted, ask before you do it. Asking has never cost anyone marks in this course.
116

[tool_result]
1	# Exporting your AI session history
2	
3	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
4	
5	Every assignment requires the complete exported history of every AI session you used. This sheet
6	explains how, for both approved agents.
7	
8	> **Export at the end of each working session, not at the end of the assignment.** Both tools have
9	> some way of losing history — a retention window, a clipboard that chokes on a long conversation.
10	> If you wait until the night before the deadline, some of what you need may no longer exist.
11	
12	> **Before you submit, open your export and read it.** Agent transcripts embed the full contents of
13	> files the agent read and the full output of commands it ran. Work in a clean folder that contains
14	> only course material.
15	
16	---
17	
18	## What goes in `sessions/`
19	
20	```
21	sessions/
22	  README.md              which agent, how many sessions, anything the export omits
23	  codex/                 one file per session
24	  claude-code/           one file per session
25	```
26	
27	You will normally have only one of those two subfolders. `sessions/README.md` is required either way
28	and can be three lines.
29	
30	---
31	
32	## 1. Codex
33	
34	### The app
35	
36	Open the session, use **Copy as Markdown** from the conversation menu, and paste into a file:
37	
38	```
39	sessions/codex/a1-session-1.md
40	```
41	
42	That is the whole procedure. Name the files in the order you worked; the names do not matter beyond
43	being distinguishable.
44	
45	Two cautions:
46	
47	- **It goes to the clipboard, not to a file.** Paste it somewhere immediately. A plain text editor is
48	  safer than Word, which has been reported to struggle with very long conversations.
49	- **Do it per session, while the session is open.** There is no bulk export.
50	
51	### The CLI
52	
53	Inside a session, `/copy` puts the conversation on the clipboard; paste it into a `.md` file the same
54	way.
55	
56	### The transcript on disk — your backup
57	
58	Codex also writes a full rollout file per session:
59	
60	```
61	~/.codex/sessions/YYYY/MM/DD/rollout-<timestamp>-<session-uuid>.jsonl
62	```
63	
64	Copy them if you want a belt-and-braces record:
65	
66	```bash
67	mkdir -p sessions/codex
68	cp ~/.codex/sessions/2026/09/11/rollout-*.jsonl sessions/codex/
69	```
70	
71	This is the complete log — more complete than the Markdown copy, which renders the conversation
72	rather than recording every tool call. **The Markdown is what I ask for. The JSONL is what saves you
73	if the Markdown copy failed and you did not notice.** Submitting both is fine and never penalised.
74	
75	Two settings to check before you start:
76	
77	- If `~/.codex/config.toml` contains `[history] persistence = "none"`, your history is being
78	  suppressed. Set it back to `"save-all"` (the default).
79	- Do not run assignment work with `--ephemeral`, which skips session persistence.
80	
81	**Check the size before you attach.** Rollout files can get large, because they store repeated
82	context snapshots and raw tool output:
83	
84	```bash
85	du -h sessions/codex/*
86	```
87	
88	If the folder is over about 50 MB, zip it and say so in `sessions/README.md`.
89	
90	---
91	
92	## 2. Claude Code
93	
94	### Option A — the raw transcript
95	
96	Transcripts are stored as JSONL at:
97	
98	```
99	~/.claude/projects/<encoded-project-path>/<session-id>.jsonl
100	```
101	
102	`<encoded-project-path>` is your working directory with every non-alphanumeric character replaced by
103	`-`. Run `/status` inside a session to get the session ID; that ID is the filename. To see every
104	session you have, `claude --resume` opens an interactive picker.
105	
106	### Option B — the readable export
107	
108	From inside a session: `/export sessions/claude-code/a1-session-1.txt`
109	
110	### Retention
111	
112	Claude Code deletes transcripts after **30 days** by default. Copy the file into your assignment
113	folder at the end of each session rather than relying on the retention setting.
114	
115	---
116	
117	## `sessions/README.md` — templates
118	
119	Ordinary case:
120	
121	```markdown
122	# AI sessions for Assignment 1
123	
124	Agent used: Codex app (4 sessions)
125	
126	- codex/ : 4 Markdown exports, complete. Rollout JSONL for the same four sessions
127	           also included.
128	```
129	
130	With a gap:
131	
132	```markdown
133	# AI sessions for Assignment 1
134	
135	Agent used: Claude Code (5 sessions)
136	
137	- claude-code/ : 4 JSONL transcripts, complete. A fifth session on 18 September
138	                 was lost before I exported it; it was about half an hour, spent
139	                 debugging the grouped-fold split, and its result is in run_a2.py.
140	```
141	
142	---
143	
144	## If a command here does not match what you see
145	
146	Both tools change quickly. If a path or a menu item on this sheet is not where it says it is,
147	**do not skip the export** — find the equivalent, do it, and say in `sessions/README.md` what you did
148	instead. Reporting that a documented step does not work is helpful and will not cost you marks.
149

1	# Submission format and checklist
2	
3	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
4	Applies to all three assignments.
5	
6	---
7	
8	## What you submit
9	
10	One archive named `lastname_firstname_aN.zip`, uploaded to Canvas by the deadline on the stated date.
11	
12	Assignments 1 and 2 are due on class days, at **6:00 PM Central** — half an hour before class, so everything is in before we meet. Assignment 3 falls on a Sunday and is due at **11:59 PM Central**. Each brief states its own deadline on the first line.
13	
14	```
15	lastname_firstname_a1/
16	  memo.pdf                  or memo.md
17	  analysis/
18	    run.sh                  or run.py — the one command
19	    README.md               what to run, what it produces, roughly how long
20	    <your code>
21	    outputs/                figures, tables, saved numbers
22	  ai_process_note.md
23	  sessions/
24	    README.md
25	    codex/  or  claude-code/
26	```
27	
28	Do not include the `data/` folder — the graders have it. Do not include virtual environments, `__pycache__`, or model checkpoints.
29	
30	---
31	
32	## The four pieces
33	
34	### `memo.pdf`
35	
36	Addressed to the business reader named in the assignment. Length limits are per assignment (see each brief; A1 carries two memo sections, A3 allows 3 pages) plus the stated number of exhibits. Exhibits do not count against the prose limit.
37	
38	Lead with the recommendation. Give the number and the number it replaces. State conditions in a form someone could check.
39	
40	### `analysis/`
41	
42	Must run end to end on a clean copy of the assignment folder with **one command**. State the command in `analysis/README.md`. If it takes more than about ten minutes, say so and say why.
43	
44	Assume the grader has Python 3.10+, numpy, pandas, scikit-learn and matplotlib and nothing else. If you need something further, list it in `analysis/README.md`. Do not require a GPU.
45	
46	Every number in your memo must come from this run.
47	
48	### `ai_process_note.md`
49	
50	One page maximum. Three things:
51	
52	1. **What you delegated.** The substantive tasks, not "helped with code."
53	2. **What you changed or rejected, and why.** Specific instances. If you rejected nothing at all across an entire assignment, say so and say why you think that was.
54	3. **How you checked the final numbers.** What you recomputed, what you compared against, what you would still not bet on.
55	
56	Generic notes score zero on this criterion. "Used Claude Code to write the analysis, reviewed the output, verified it was correct" is a generic note.
57	
58	### `sessions/`
59	
60	Complete exported history of every AI session used, per `03_exporting_your_session_history.md`, with a `README.md` naming the agent and disclosing anything the exports omit.
61	
62	---
63	
64	## Checklist
65	
66	**Analysis**
67	
68	- [ ] Runs from a clean copy of the folder with one command
69	- [ ] No absolute paths from your machine
70	- [ ] No dependency the grader will not have, or it is listed
71	- [ ] Random seeds set, so the numbers reproduce
72	- [ ] Every memo number appears in the run output or in `analysis/outputs/`
73	
74	**Memo**
75	
76	- [ ] Within the length limit
77	- [ ] Recommendation in the first paragraph
78	- [ ] Every required exhibit present and numbered as the brief numbers it
79	- [ ] Uncertainty stated once, plainly
80	- [ ] Any condition on the recommendation is checkable
81	- [ ] Addressed to the reader the brief names
82	
83	**Process note**
84	
85	- [ ] Names specific delegated tasks
86	- [ ] Names at least one specific thing you corrected or rejected, with the reason
87	- [ ] Says how the final numbers were verified, not just that they were
88	
89	**Sessions**
90	
91	- [ ] Every session exported
92	- [ ] Exports are unedited
93	- [ ] `sessions/README.md` names the agent and any gaps
94	- [ ] You have opened the export and confirmed it contains nothing private
95	
96	**Yourself**
97	
98	- [ ] You can explain any part of the submission out loud, without notes
99	- [ ] You could reconstruct the central argument on paper, with no tools
100	- [ ] There is no number in the memo you could not defend if asked where it came from
101	
102	---
103	
104	## Late work
105	
106	Per the course outline: within 24 hours, −20%. 24 to 48 hours, −40%. Beyond 48 hours, no credit. Extensions for serious circumstances must be arranged in advance where possible.
107	
108	A partial submission on time is worth substantially more than a complete one two days late. If you are running short, submit the memo and the analysis you have, and say in the process note what is missing.
109	
110	---
111	
112	## Ownership checks
113	
114	The instructor may ask you to explain a piece of your submitted work, or to make a small modification to it, in class or in a brief oral check. These are short and are not adversarial. They exist so that assignment credit reflects your own judgment. Prepare for them by not submitting anything you do not understand.
115

1	# Setting up your coding agent
2	
3	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
4	Do this in the first week, before Assignment 1. It takes about twenty minutes.
5	
6	---
7	
8	## The short version
9	
10	**This course uses one of two coding agents: the Codex app or Claude Code.**
11	**No other coding agent may be used on course work.**
12	**Codex has a free path, so nobody needs to spend money to complete an assignment.**
13	
14	---
15	
16	## 1. Choosing between the two
17	
18	Either is fine, and you do not need both. Pick one and stay with it for an assignment; switching
19	mid-assignment just makes your `sessions/` folder harder to assemble.
20	
21	| | **Codex** | **Claude Code** |
22	|---|---|---|
23	| Cost | **Free path.** The Free plan plus the $100 student credit grant, below. | Requires a Claude plan that includes it, or pay-as-you-go API credits. |
24	| Form | Desktop app, CLI, or IDE extension | CLI |
25	| Transcript export | *Copy as Markdown* from the conversation menu, one click | `/export`, plus a complete JSONL transcript on disk |
26	
27	**If cost matters at all, use Codex.** The free plan and the student grant cover this course
28	comfortably, and nothing in any assignment needs more than that.
29	
30	## 2. What you need
31	
32	1. **For Codex:** a ChatGPT account — the **Free plan is sufficient** — and the Codex app or CLI.
33	2. **For Claude Code:** a Claude account on a plan that includes Claude Code, and the CLI installed.
34	3. Either way: Python 3.10+ with numpy, pandas, scikit-learn and matplotlib.
35	
36	## 3. Claim the student credits (Codex)
37	
38	OpenAI grants **$100 in Codex credits to verified students** at degree-granting universities in the
39	US and Canada. UIC qualifies.
40	
41	1. Sign in to ChatGPT, then start verification from the Codex for Students page
42	   (`developers.openai.com/community/students`).
43	2. Verify with your **@uic.edu** address. Verification is handled by SheerID and is usually instant.
44	3. The credits are applied automatically to that account.
45	
46	Three things to know:
47	
48	- **They expire twelve months after the grant date.** That is well past the end of this course.
49	- **They are ChatGPT credits usable in Codex**, not API credits. You do not need an API key, and you
50	  should not create one for this course.
51	- **One grant per student**, and it is tied to the account you verified with. Verify on the account
52	  you will actually use.
53	
54	**If verification fails** — you are outside the US or Canada at the moment you claim, your school
55	type is not recognised, or anything else — **email me.** Do not buy a subscription to work around it.
56	
57	## 4. Verify the workflow
58	
59	Do the setup exercise in `setup_exercise/` before Assignment 1. It is ungraded, takes about fifteen
60	minutes, and checks three things:
61	
62	- Python runs and has the packages the course needs;
63	- your agent runs and can read a file in the folder you are working in;
64	- you can export a session transcript and find it afterwards.
65	
66	If any of the three does not work, that is what the exercise is for. Bring it to me in week two, not
67	the night before Assignment 1 is due.
68	
69	## 5. Staying inside the budget
70	
71	Free-plan usage plus the grant covers the three assignments comfortably, but that assumes you are
72	not wasting it. Four habits, in order of how much they matter:
73	
74	**Never let the agent read a data file into the conversation.** `member_months.csv` is 33,000 rows.
75	Pasting it, or letting the agent `cat` it, can burn more of your allowance in one message than a
76	whole working session should. Have the agent *write code that reads the file* and report back the
77	numbers. The data dictionaries are short and are meant to be read; the CSVs are not.
78	
79	**Match the model to the task.** Use a cheaper model for routine work — writing a loop, fixing an
80	import, reformatting a table. Save the strongest model for the things that need it: debugging
81	something you do not understand, or arguing with you about an evaluation design. The difference in
82	cost between the top and bottom models is more than twentyfold, and most of what you will ask for
83	does not need the top one.
84	
85	**Start a new session for each work block, and keep it on one topic.** A single session that runs
86	all week re-sends its whole history with every message. Three focused sessions cost far less than
87	one sprawling one, and they are easier to export and easier for me to read.
88	
89	**Don't re-run the expensive things.** Cache what you compute. `analyst_model.py` produces the same
90	numbers every time; run it once and save the output rather than asking the agent to re-run it in
91	every session.
92	
93	**Check your balance after Assignment 1.** If you are more than a third of the way through the
94	grant, tell me — either you are working in a way that is costing you unnecessarily and I can help,
95	or my estimate was wrong and I need to know.
96	
97	**If you run out, email me.** 
98	
99	## 6. Why only these two
100	
101	Not because nothing else works. Because when everyone uses one of two tools, the setup instructions
102	apply to everyone, the transcript format is one I can read quickly, a question in class about "why
103	did it do that" has an answer that helps the whole room, and the audit trail every submission
104	depends on is one I can actually verify. Both approved tools produce a complete, exportable session
105	history; that is the property the course requires and it is not universal.
106

1	# `member_months.csv` — data dictionary
2	
3	Extract prepared by the Membership Analytics team, **March 2026**.
4	Source systems: Billing (Zuora), Product Analytics (app event stream), Zendesk, Salesforce.
5	
6	**Grain.** One row per **member per calendar month** in which the member was active at any point during that month. A member appears once for every month they were on the book. Members who cancel stop appearing after the month in which they cancelled.
7	
8	**Window.** January 2024 through December 2025. All outcomes have matured: the last snapshot month is December 2025, whose 60-day outcome window closed at the end of February 2026, before this extract was pulled.
9	
10	---
11	
12	## Columns
13	
14	| Column | Type | Source | Definition |
15	|---|---|---|---|
16	| `member_id` | int | Billing | Stable member identifier. |
17	| `snapshot_month` | str | Billing | The calendar month the row describes. *Note: this field was written by three different pipeline versions over the extract window and the formatting is not consistent.* |
18	| `signup_month` | str | Billing | Month the member's first paid subscription started. |
19	| `acquisition_channel` | str | Salesforce | How the member was acquired: `organic`, `paid_search`, `partner_gym`, `corporate`. The `corporate` channel covers members enrolled through an employer wellness contract; the first such contract went live in February 2025. |
20	| `plan_tier` | str | Billing | `basic`, `plus`, or `elite`. |
21	| `monthly_price` | float | Billing | List price of the plan in USD. Does not reflect promotional discounts. |
22	| `city_tier` | str | Salesforce | `metro`, `suburban`, `rural`. Self-reported at signup. |
23	| `tenure_months` | int | Billing | Completed months since `signup_month`, as of the start of `snapshot_month`. |
24	| `sessions_last_30d` | int | Product Analytics | Completed workout sessions logged in the 30 days ending on the **last day of the previous month**. |
25	| `app_opens_last_30d` | float | Product Analytics | App launches over the same 30-day window. |
26	| `avg_session_minutes` | float | Product Analytics | Mean session length over the same window. |
27	| `days_since_last_login` | float | Product Analytics | Days between the member's last app login and the **last day of the previous month**. Capped at 90. |
28	| `support_tickets_last_30d` | int | Zendesk | Tickets opened by the member over the same 30-day window. |
29	| `payment_failures_last_90d` | int | Billing | Declined or failed charge attempts in the 90 days ending on the **last day of the previous month**. A failure is recorded when the card issuer declines; it does not imply the member intended to cancel. |
30	| `referrals_made` | int | Product Analytics | Successful referrals, same 30-day window. |
31	| `winback_offer_sent` | int | Salesforce | `1` if the Retention team contacted this member about this month. **The Retention team works from the at-risk list that Membership Analytics circulates on the third business day after month close.** |
32	| `exit_survey_started` | int | Product Analytics | `1` if the member opened the cancellation exit survey. |
33	| `churn_next_60d` | int | Billing | **Target.** `1` if the member's subscription terminated within 60 days of the end of `snapshot_month`. |
34	
35	---
36	
37	## Known issues logged against this extract
38	
39	- **NLF-2231** — The March 2025 load was re-run after a partition failure. The re-run was not idempotent. *Status: open.*
40	- **NLF-2198** — `snapshot_month` formatting varies by pipeline version. *Status: won't fix, downstream consumers handle it.*
41	
42	## Notes from the analyst who pulled it
43	
44	> Left all columns in — figured the model can decide what's useful. Didn't do anything about NLF-2231, shouldn't matter for modelling. Corporate members are a small slice so I didn't look at them separately.
45

USER  [2026-09-17T20:21:58.199Z]

[tool_result]
1	"""
2	Assignment 1 starter.  Northline Fitness churn.
3	
4	This file exists so you do not have to retype boilerplate.  It deliberately
5	does NOT clean the data, choose features, or drop any column.  Every one of
6	those is a decision you are being graded on.
7	
8	The evaluation windows below are the ones the brief tells you to use.  They
9	are here so your A3 numbers are comparable to everyone else's -- you are
10	graded on explaining why this shape is right, not on inventing the boundaries.
11	
12	Usage:
13	    from load import load_raw, month_key, TRAIN_END, VALID_MONTH, TEST_START
14	    df = load_raw()
15	    df["month"] = df["snapshot_month"].map(month_key)
16	"""
17	from pathlib import Path
18	
19	import pandas as pd
20	
21	DATA = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "data" / "member_months.csv"
22	
23	# The economics from the assignment brief.
24	OFFER_COST = 18.0        # cost of extending one retention offer, USD
25	MEMBER_LTV = 240.0       # value of a member who stays, USD
26	OFFER_CONVERSION = 0.35  # fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer
27	
28	TARGET = "churn_next_60d"
29	
30	# The evaluation windows from the brief, as 'YYYY-MM' strings.
31	# Training  : months <= TRAIN_END
32	# Not used  : 2025-07, 2025-08   (labels for the last training month mature at
33	#                                 the end of August 2025)
34	# Validation: month == VALID_MONTH
35	# Test      : months >= TEST_START   -- touch this once, at the end
36	TRAIN_END = "2025-06"
37	VALID_MONTH = "2025-09"
38	TEST_START = "2025-10"
39	
40	
41	def load_raw() -> pd.DataFrame:
42	    """Read the extract exactly as delivered.  No cleaning."""
43	    return pd.read_csv(DATA)
44	
45	
46	def month_key(value: str) -> str:
47	    """Normalise the three `snapshot_month` formats to 'YYYY-MM'.
48	
49	    You still have to decide whether and how to use the month.  This just
50	    stops the three formats from silently becoming three different categories.
51	    """
52	    s = str(value).strip()
53	    if "/" in s:                      # 'MM/YYYY'
54	        month, year = s.split("/")
55	        return f"{year}-{int(month):02d}"
56	    return s[:7]                      # 'YYYY-MM' or 'YYYY-MM-DD'
57	
58	
59	def offer_value(churned: int, offered: int) -> float:
60	    """Realised dollar value of one targeting decision, per the brief."""
61	    if not offered:
62	        return 0.0
63	    return MEMBER_LTV * OFFER_CONVERSION - OFFER_COST if churned else -OFFER_COST
64	
65	
66	if __name__ == "__main__":
67	    df = load_raw()
68	    df["month"] = df["snapshot_month"].map(month_key)
69	    print(f"rows                {len(df):,}")
70	    print(f"unique member_id    {df['member_id'].nunique():,}")
71	    print(f"columns             {len(df.columns) - 1}")
72	    print(f"target base rate    {df[TARGET].mean():.4f}")
73	    print()
74	    print("months present:")
75	    print(sorted(df["month"].unique()))
76	    print()
77	    print(f"rows in training window (<= {TRAIN_END}) : "
78	          f"{(df['month'] <= TRAIN_END).sum():,}")
79	    print(f"rows in validation window ({VALID_MONTH})  : "
80	          f"{(df['month'] == VALID_MONTH).sum():,}")
81	    print(f"rows in test window (>= {TEST_START})     : "
82	          f"{(df['month'] >= TEST_START).sum():,}")
83

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T20:21:58.568Z]
==============================================================================

[tool_result]
1	"""
2	The junior analyst's pipeline, exactly as they ran it.
3	
4	This file is given to you so that you do not have to reverse-engineer the
5	headline number.  It runs, it reproduces the figures quoted in the brief, and
6	it is wrong.  Your job is to find out *why* it is wrong -- not to rebuild it.
7	
8	Read it.  Every line is a defensible-looking choice, and several of them are
9	the assignment.  Note in particular that the modelling itself is careful: the
10	encoder and the scaler are fitted inside each fold, so there is no
11	preprocessing leak here.  Whatever is wrong with this number is wrong at the
12	level of the protocol, not the pipeline.
13	
14	    python3 analyst_model.py
15	
16	Expected output (Python 3.10+, scikit-learn >= 1.3; small version drift is
17	normal and does not matter):
18	
19	    rows                       33,340
20	    5-fold cross-validated AUC  0.967
21	    5-fold cross-validated AP   0.899
22	    estimated programme value   $19,774 per quarter
23	"""
24	import warnings
25	from pathlib import Path
26	
27	import numpy as np
28	import pandas as pd
29	from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
30	from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
31	from sklearn.metrics import average_precision_score, roc_auc_score
32	from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold
33	from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
34	from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder, StandardScaler
35	
36	warnings.filterwarnings("ignore")
37	
38	DATA = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "data" / "member_months.csv"
39	
40	# Economics from the finance team, per the brief.
41	OFFER_COST = 18.0        # cost of extending one retention offer, USD
42	MEMBER_LTV = 240.0       # value of a member who stays, USD
43	OFFER_CONVERSION = 0.35  # fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer
44	
45	TARGET = "churn_next_60d"
46	# The analyst dropped only the identifier and the two date strings, on the
47	# grounds that they are not numeric features.  Everything else was kept:
48	# "figured the model can decide what's useful."
49	NOT_FEATURES = (TARGET, "member_id", "snapshot_month", "signup_month")
50	
51	
52	def analyst_pipeline(categorical, numeric) -> Pipeline:
53	    """One-hot the categoricals, standardise the rest, fit logistic regression.
54	
55	    Everything fitted from data lives inside this object, so when it is fitted
56	    on a fold's training portion the encoder and the scaler see only that
57	    portion.  This much the analyst got right.
58	    """
59	    return Pipeline([
60	        ("prep", ColumnTransformer([
61	            ("cat", OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore", drop="first"), categorical),
62	            ("num", StandardScaler(), numeric),
63	        ])),
64	        ("est", LogisticRegression(max_iter=2000)),
65	    ])
66	
67	
68	def main() -> None:
69	    df = pd.read_csv(DATA)
70	    y = df[TARGET].to_numpy()
71	
72	    features = [c for c in df.columns if c not in NOT_FEATURES]
73	    categorical = [c for c in features if not pd.api.types.is_numeric_dtype(df[c])]
74	    numeric = [c for c in features if c not in categorical]
75	    X = df[features]
76	
77	    folds = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=0)
78	    oof = np.zeros(len(df))
79	    for train_idx, test_idx in folds.split(X, y):
80	        model = analyst_pipeline(categorical, numeric)
81	        model.fit(X.iloc[train_idx], y[train_idx])
82	        oof[test_idx] = model.predict_proba(X.iloc[test_idx])[:, 1]
83	
84	    auc = roc_auc_score(y, oof)
85	    ap = average_precision_score(y, oof)
86	
87	    # Value of one targeting decision: offer a churner and you net
88	    # LTV * conversion - cost; offer a non-churner and you lose the cost.
89	    realised = y * MEMBER_LTV * OFFER_CONVERSION - OFFER_COST
90	    grid = np.linspace(0.005, 0.6, 400)
91	    values = [float(np.sum((oof > t) * realised)) for t in grid]
92	    best = int(np.argmax(values))
93	    # 24 months of data = 8 quarters.
94	    per_quarter = values[best] / 8.0
95	
96	    print(f"rows                        {len(df):,}")
97	    print(f"unique members              {df['member_id'].nunique():,}")
98	    print(f"features used               {len(features)}")
99	    print(f"base rate                   {y.mean():.4f}")
100	    print()
101	    print(f"5-fold cross-validated AUC  {auc:.3f}")
102	    print(f"5-fold cross-validated AP   {ap:.3f}")
103	    print(f"optimal threshold           {grid[best]:.3f}")
104	    print(f"estimated programme value   ${per_quarter:,.0f} per quarter")
105	    print()
106	    print("Recommendation: strong signal, move to production this quarter.")
107	
108	
109	if __name__ == "__main__":
110	    main()
111

1	# AI sessions for Assignment 1
2	
3	Agent used: Claude Code (VS Code extension, on Windows 11)
4	
5	- `claude-code/` : one `.jsonl` and one `.txt` per session.
6	
7	## How these were exported, and why not with `/export`
8	
9	`03_exporting_your_session_history.md` gives two options for Claude Code:
10	Option A, the raw JSONL at `~/.claude/projects/<encoded-path>/<session-id>.jsonl`,
11	and Option B, `/export` from inside a session.
12	
13	**Option B is not available in the VS Code extension.** Running
14	`/export sessions/claude-code/a1-session-1.txt` there returns "/export isn't
15	available in this environment". The `claude` CLI has no `export` subcommand
16	either (checked against `claude --help` on 2026-09-17, CLI version 2.52-era
17	build). So, per the sheet's closing instruction to find the equivalent and say
18	what was done instead:
19	
20	1. **Option A, unchanged.** The raw transcript is copied verbatim to
21	   `claude-code/a1-session-N.jsonl`. This is the complete record.
22	2. **Option B, reproduced.** `render_transcript.py` in this folder renders that
23	   JSONL as readable text at `claude-code/a1-session-N.txt`. It writes out every
24	   user and assistant message, every tool call with its arguments, and every
25	   tool result in full, with nothing truncated. Run it with:
26	
27	       python sessions/render_transcript.py \
28	           sessions/claude-code/a1-session-1.jsonl \
29	           sessions/claude-code/a1-session-1.txt
30	
31	Both files are submitted. The JSONL is authoritative; the `.txt` is for reading.
32	
33	## Known gap
34	
35	Each transcript is copied from disk near the end of its session, so it stops a
36	few messages short of the true end. The missing tail is the export and the
37	`git commit`/`git push` that followed it, which are visible in the repository
38	history. Nothing about the analysis is lost this way.
39	
40	## Sessions
41	
42	| # | Date | Machine | Topic |
43	|---|---|---|---|
44	| 1 | 2026-09-17 | Windows 11 | Setup only. Folder audit, Python environment, `.gitignore` fix, `NOTES.md`. No analysis. |
45

[tool_result]
1	"""Render a Claude Code JSONL transcript as readable text.
2	
3	The VS Code extension has no /export command (checked 2026-09-17; the CLI has
4	no export subcommand either), so this reproduces Option B of
5	course_docs/03_exporting_your_session_history.md from the Option A raw file.
6	
7	Usage:
8	    python sessions/render_transcript.py <session>.jsonl <output>.txt
9	
10	Nothing is truncated: tool calls and their full output are included, matching
11	what /export would have written.
12	"""
13	import json
14	import sys
15	from pathlib import Path
16	
17	RULE = "=" * 78
18	
19	
20	def render_block(block):
21	    """One content block -> list of text lines."""
22	    kind = block.get("type")
23	    if kind == "text":
24	        return [block.get("text", "")]
25	    if kind == "thinking":
26	        return ["[thinking]", block.get("thinking", "")]
27	    if kind == "tool_use":
28	        args = json.dumps(block.get("input", {}), indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)
29	        return ["[tool_use: %s]" % block.get("name", "?"), args]
30	    if kind == "tool_result":
31	        content = block.get("content", "")
32	        if isinstance(content, list):
33	            parts = []
34	            for item in content:
35	                if isinstance(item, dict) and item.get("type") == "text":
36	                    parts.append(item.get("text", ""))
37	                else:
38	                    parts.append(json.dumps(item, ensure_ascii=False))
39	            content = "\n".join(parts)
40	        return ["[tool_result]", str(content)]
41	    return ["[%s]" % kind, json.dumps(block, ensure_ascii=False)]
42	
43	
44	def render_message(record):
45	    """One JSONL record -> list of text lines, or [] to skip."""
46	    role = record.get("type")
47	    if role not in ("user", "assistant", "system"):
48	        return []
49	    message = record.get("message") or {}
50	    content = message.get("content", record.get("content", ""))
51	    lines = []
52	    if isinstance(content, str):
53	        lines.append(content)
54	    elif isinstance(content, list):
55	        for block in content:
56	            if isinstance(block, dict):
57	                lines.extend(render_block(block))
58	            else:
59	                lines.append(str(block))
60	    if not any(line.strip() for line in lines):
61	        return []
62	    header = "%s  [%s]" % (role.upper(), record.get("timestamp", "no timestamp"))
63	    return [RULE, header, RULE, ""] + lines + [""]
64	
65	
66	def main():
67	    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
68	        sys.exit(__doc__)
69	    src, dest = Path(sys.argv[1]), Path(sys.argv[2])
70	    out, kept, skipped = [], 0, 0
71	    with src.open(encoding="utf-8") as handle:
72	        for line in handle:
73	            line = line.strip()
74	            if not line:
75	                continue
76	            try:
77	                record = json.loads(line)
78	            except json.JSONDecodeError:
79	                skipped += 1
80	                continue
81	            rendered = render_message(record)
82	            if rendered:
83	                out.extend(rendered)
84	                kept += 1
85	    dest.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
86	    dest.write_text("\n".join(out), encoding="utf-8")
87	    print("%s -> %s" % (src.name, dest))
88	    print("messages rendered: %d, unparseable lines: %d" % (kept, skipped))
89	
90	
91	if __name__ == "__main__":
92	    main()
93

1	# Cascade Outdoor — data dictionary
2	
3	Extract prepared by Merchandising Analytics, September 2026.
4	
5	Both divisions run on the same planning platform and record the same twelve fields with the same definitions. The extracts differ in size, not in structure.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## `division_h.csv` and `division_a.csv` — the training extracts
10	
11	**Grain.** One row per store per week. **Target: `units`.**
12	
13	| Column | Definition |
14	|---|---|
15	| `store_id` | Store identifier, 1–60. |
16	| `week_id` | Sequential identifier for the store-week, assigned in extract order and unique to one store-week. |
17	| `promo_depth_pct` | Discount off list, 0–45. |
18	| `own_price_index` | Cascade's shelf price for the category, indexed to 100 = plan. |
19	| `competitor_price_index` | Matched competitor price on the same basis, from a third-party scrape. |
20	| `marketing_spend_k` | Division marketing spend attributed to the store-week, $000. |
21	| `temp_f` | Mean weekly temperature at the store, °F. |
22	| `loyalty_share` | Share of division sales in that store-week on a loyalty account. |
23	| `store_sqft_k` | Store selling area, thousands of sq ft. |
24	| `inventory_cover_wks` | Weeks of forward cover at the start of the week. |
25	| `assortment_breadth` | Distinct SKUs carried in the category that week. |
26	| `catalog_pages` | Pages the category held in that period's catalogue. |
27	| `returns_rate` | Units returned / units sold, trailing 8 weeks. |
28	| `staffing_hours` | Scheduled floor hours in the category that week. |
29	| `units` | Units sold. |
30	
31	The twelve predictor fields were specified by a single working group in 2024 and are populated by the same nightly job for both divisions. Nobody has audited which of them actually matter.
32	
33	### Sizes
34	
35	| | Rows in file | Distinct store-weeks |
36	|---|---:|---:|
37	| Hardgoods | 3,350 | see the pipeline note below |
38	| **Apparel** | **197** | 197 |
39	
40	> **Why Apparel is small.** Apparel migrated to the new planning platform in March. The legacy system recorded `promo_depth_pct` net of employee discount and `loyalty_share` on a different denominator, so pre-migration weeks are not comparable and were excluded. Merchandising Analytics expects Apparel to reach parity with Hardgoods on volume of usable history by late 2027.
41	
42	> **Pipeline note (CO-4471, open).** The Hardgoods weekly load was re-run in August after a scheduler fault. **The re-run appended rather than replaced.** Merchandising Analytics has confirmed the fault; the extract has not been regenerated, and `division_h.csv` is shipped as the pipeline produced it. Every affected store-week appears in the file twice, identical on every column including `units`.
43	
44	> **Apparel cleaning note.** Unlike Hardgoods, the Apparel extract has been cleaned before delivery. The divisional planner flagged three weeks and Merchandising Analytics adjudicated them; **all three were removed** and the file you have contains the remaining 197. For the record:
45	>
46	> | Week | What happened | Decision |
47	> |---|---|---|
48	> | `50116` | Store shut for a burst pipe. The system still wrote a row, with `units = 0`. | Removed. Not a demand observation — nothing was on sale that week. |
49	> | `50088` | `units` recorded as 925 against a store average near 110. Confirmed as a keying error: the true figure was entered with an extra digit. | Removed. A real week with a corrupted target; the original value could not be recovered from the source system. |
50	> | `50151` | Black Friday. `promo_depth_pct` 44.8 and `marketing_spend_k` 61.4, both far outside any other week in the file. | Removed. Real demand, but from a promotional regime the weekly planning model is not intended to cover. Merchandising Analytics plans a separate peak-week model. |
51	>
52	> You do not need to revisit these decisions. They are recorded here so that you know what the extract is and is not.
53	
54	---
55	
56	## `replicates.csv` — controlled repeat observations, and the final test extract
57	
58	**This is not additional training data and it is not a third division.** It has two uses in this assignment, both described in the brief: it is where σ̂² comes from, and it is the extract on which final models are scored once.
59	
60	During the 2025 planning-system validation, Merchandising Analytics ran a controlled exercise. In each division they identified 45 store-week configurations — distinct combinations of the twelve fields above — and located **ten independent weeks that matched each configuration exactly on every recorded field**. None of these weeks appears in `division_h.csv` or `division_a.csv`.
61	
62	So every group of ten rows sharing a `config_id` is identical on every column except `units`.
63	
64	| Column | Definition |
65	|---|---|
66	| `division` | `H` or `A`. |
67	| `config_id` | Configuration identifier. Ten rows share each one. |
68	| `replicate` | 1–10 within the configuration. |
69	| *(the twelve predictor fields)* | As above. Identical within a `config_id`. |
70	| `units` | Units sold in that week. |
71	
72	The exercise was commissioned to settle a question the planning team kept arguing about: *how much of the week-to-week variation in units is even forecastable, and is the answer the same in both divisions?*
73
