Pulled and verified. Three things before the A3 design.

**Cross-machine reproducibility holds.** Re-running everything on the Mac leaves `a2_table.csv` and all six A1 outputs byte-identical to what Windows committed. Only `a2_test_scores.csv` moved, by at most **4×10⁻¹⁴** in `p_churn` — ARM vs x86 floating-point noise, identical to 10 decimal places. It matters for A3 that I checked the thing that could have bitten: **the same 203 rows** fall above the cost threshold on both machines, so the dollar figure will not depend on which laptop ran it. Worth a line in the process note.

**Schedule.** Due Thursday 1 Oct, 6:00 PM Central — two days. A3 is the last 20 points of Part A; Part B is 35 points and untouched, Handout 3 unread, and neither `memo.md` nor `ai_process_note.md` is started. That's your call to make, not mine, but it argues for keeping A3 tight today rather than exploring every option below.

## A3 design — your decisions

Item 1 is already done: test AUC overall 0.644 (4,556 rows, 408 churners), corporate 0.404 (860, 34), other 0.641 (3,696, 374), all from A2 row 3/4. Item 2 is arithmetic: offering is worth 66p − 18(1−p) = 84p − 18, which beats 0 when **p > 18/84 = 0.2143**. Neither needs a decision. These do:

**D1 — What counts as one offer.** The program scores the active book monthly, so a member present in all three test months can be offered three times. (a) Every qualifying member-month is an offer — matches the brief's per-offer economics and the monthly program. (b) At most one offer per member across the window. (c) (a) headline, (b) as a one-line sensitivity. *I'd take (a)*; it's the unit everything else in Part A uses.

**D2 — Reporting period.** The test window is exactly one quarter. (a) One figure for Oct–Dec — directly comparable to the analyst's $19,774/quarter. (b) Per month. (c) Quarter headline plus the monthly split. *I'd take (c)*: the monthly split is three numbers and shows whether the value is stable.

**D3 — What to price alongside it.** Required: t* versus offer-everyone. Optional additions: no program ($0, the do-nothing floor — R1 §1's "distance from something"); the analyst's 0.267 threshold; the full value-versus-threshold curve. The curve is the interesting one and the risky one: plotting it is *reporting*, not selecting, since t* was derived from the finance table before anything was scored — but it will look like test-set shopping unless the memo is explicit. *I'd take t\* + offer-everyone + $0, and add the analyst's 0.267 only if you want to make the "derived versus grid-searched" point numerically.*

**D4 — Calibration, and where.** The threshold multiplies p by money, so it assumes p is a real probability, not just a ranking (R1 §4.7). The validation month (Sep 2025) is still unused and is exactly the place for this — it is not the test set. (a) Check calibration on Sep 2025, report it as a caveat, leave t* as derived. (b) Check on the test window itself, reporting only. (c) Skip it. *I'd take (a).* It needs one addition to `a2_evaluation.py` — save validation-month scores alongside the test scores — so that A3 still never rescores the test set.

**D5 — Segment dollars.** Overall only, or overall plus corporate/other? Corporate ranks below a coin flip, so its dollar line may well be negative, and the book is forecast at 35% corporate. *I'd report both* — it's the evidence your recommendation's condition will rest on.

**D6 — The recommendation.** Yours entirely. The brief wants a condition someone at Northline could check: a share of the book, or a measured AUC. Worth deciding before you see the dollar figure, so the number doesn't write the verdict for you.

Three things I'll carry into the caveats whatever you choose: the test quarter is Oct–Dec (8.4–9.5% base rate) but deployment is Apr–Jun (5.3–7.1% in 2025), so there are fewer churners to find; `winback_offer_sent` is 1 on 1,294 non-churners, meaning part of the history is already post-treatment; and the analyst's $19,774 used a threshold grid-searched on leaked out-of-fold scores, so it isn't a like-for-like comparison.

Confirmed, and it matches the brief exactly:

| Brief's window | Months | What A2 actually did |
|---|---|---|
| Training, 2024-01 → 2025-06 | 18 months | **Fitted on.** 21,031 rows, 1,767 churners — rows 0–2 cross-validate inside it, row 3 fits on all of it |
| Not used, 2025-07, 2025-08 | 2 months | **In neither partition** |
| Validation, 2025-09 | 1 month | **In neither partition** — A2 made no choice by looking at data, so it was never touched |
| Test, 2025-10 → 2025-12 | 3 months | **Scored once**, at row 3; row 4 re-splits those same scores |

No month appears in both fit and scored; all 24 months are accounted for; the constants come from the supplied `starter/load.py` rather than being retyped.

**One interpretive choice worth knowing you made**, since it's the only place the brief leaves room. The brief says rows before change 3 are "cross-validated on the building data" without defining "building data." Session 3 read it as the training window (≤ 2025-06). The alternative reading is "everything that isn't test," i.e. 2024-01 → 2025-09. Your reading is the more conservative one — it keeps the gap months genuinely unused and leaves the validation month intact — and it's the only reading consistent with the table's own labels. The cost is that row 0 differs from the analyst's printed 0.967 in two ways at once (deduplication *and* the shorter window), which the A2 draft already footnotes. Row 0 came out at 0.968, so restricting the window cost nothing in practice.

I mean any future change to that file — by either of us — that alters which rows go into which partition without anyone noticing. Concretely, the partitions are built on two lines, [a2_evaluation.py:185-186](analysis/part1/a2_evaluation.py#L185-L186):

```python
build = df[df["month"] <= TRAIN_END].reset_index(drop=True)
test  = df[df["month"] >= TEST_START].reset_index(drop=True)
```

Things that would quietly break compliance:

- **A3 needs validation-month scores** (decision D4). The natural way to add that is another filter line in this file. Write `<=` where you meant `==`, or reuse `build` by mistake, and the validation month silently joins the training data. Nothing would error; the AUCs would just shift a little and you'd have no reason to look.
- **Deciding to fit on more data.** If a later session reads "building data" the other way and changes `TRAIN_END` to `VALID_MONTH`, the gap months and the validation month enter the fit, the test window is still scored, and every A2 number changes without anything announcing it.
- **A refactor.** If A3 ends up importing the fitting logic from here and passing its own frame, the guarantee that `build` and `test` never overlap moves out of this file and stops being checked anywhere.

The assertions would be three lines that fail loudly instead:

```python
assert set(build["month"]) & set(test["month"]) == set()
assert {"2025-07", "2025-08", VALID_MONTH} & set(build["month"]) == set()
assert sorted(test["month"].unique()) == ["2025-10", "2025-11", "2025-12"]
```

They cost nothing at runtime and they encode the brief's table as executable fact rather than as a comment. They're also the honest answer if a grader or the professor asks "how do you know nothing leaked from the validation month into your fit?" — the run would have crashed.

It's a small thing and entirely optional. Say the word and I'll add it as part of the A3 work; otherwise I'll leave the file as it is.

The test window's 4,556 rows come from only **1,669 distinct members** — 1,376 of them appear in all three months. So the same person shows up repeatedly, and the question is whether an "offer" is a row or a person.

At the threshold, **203 rows** are above 0.2143, and those rows belong to **166 distinct members**: 132 flagged once, 31 flagged twice, 3 flagged in all three months.

So the decision is literally what you multiply by $18:

- **(a) One offer per member-month — 203 offers.** The program as the brief describes it "scores every active member at the start of each month" and sends the top scorers an offer. A member who still looks risky in November gets contacted again in November. This is the operating reality of a monthly program.
- **(b) One offer per member — 166 offers.** You'd refuse to re-contact someone you already gave a month at 50% off.

Two reasons I lean to (a) beyond it matching the program description. First, the brief's economics are defined per offer: $18 each, $66 if it lands on a would-be churner. Second — and this is the awkward part of (b) — the label lives on one specific row. A churner has exactly one flagged row, their final month. So a member flagged in October and November who cancels in December generates, under (a), two wasted offers at −$18 and one good offer at +$66. Under (b) you'd have to pick *which* of their three months the single offer happened in, and if you picked October you'd score it as a wasted offer on a member who did in fact churn. That's an artefact of the accounting, not a real business fact.

Where (b) earns its place is as a one-line sensitivity: 37 of the 203 offers are repeat contacts, so a "contact each member at most once per quarter" policy would cost $666 less in offers and forgo whatever those repeats caught. That's cheap to report and pre-empts the obvious question from the VP.

The related judgment, which is yours and which the memo should probably name: the $240 LTV is a *lifetime* value, so if an offer retains someone in October, counting a second $66 for retaining them again in December would be double-counting. Under (a) that can't happen — only one row per churner carries the label — but it's worth being able to say why.

**The analyst has no test window.** Their script cross-validates over all 24 months with random folds and computes value on the out-of-fold scores for all 33,340 rows, then divides the total by 8 quarters ([analyst_model.py:88-94](part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py#L88-L94)). 1. **Their number as they computed it** — $19,774/quarter, leaked scores, grid-searched threshold, averaged over 24 months. Quotable as the figure we're replacing, but not comparable line-by-line with ours.
2. **Our scores, their threshold 0.267.** Same model, same test window, same 4,556 scores — only the cut point changes. This isolates what their *threshold-picking method* costs. Free: it re-reads scores we already have.
3. **Their model scored on our test window.** This would isolate what the leaky columns cost in dollars. But it means fitting a second model and scoring the test window a second time — and in A2 you already set the norm the other way ("no single-column reference on the test window: score once honoured"). **Your fourth question is D6, the recommendation** (D4 is still the open one — calibration). On its substance, and this is the part I'd push back on:

"Assuming finance's numbers are accurate, expect $X per quarter" is a **forecast**, and the test window doesn't support it. What the test window supports is a *realised* claim: "on Oct–Dec 2025, this model at this threshold would have returned $X." Three things break the projection to April 2026:

- **Season.** Oct–Dec ran 8.4–9.5% churn; Apr–Jun 2025 ran 5.3–7.1%. Fewer churners to find means less value available, before the model is even considered.
- **Composition.** Corporate was 19% of test rows and is forecast at 35% of the April book — and corporate is the segment the model ranks at 0.404.
- **The offer itself.** `winback_offer_sent` is 1 on 1,294 non-churners in the history, so some members were already treated; the retention effect of a *new* programme on top of that isn't what the historical outcomes measure.

So the honest shape is: state the realised quarter figure, then state what would have to hold for it to carry forward. That's also exactly what the brief asks for — "state the condition so that someone at Northline could check whether it holds."

The plausible shapes, for you to choose between once you see the numbers:

- **Deploy, conditionally on composition** — run it, but only while corporate stays under some share of the book, and check that share monthly.
- **Deploy on a segment** — run it on non-corporate members, exclude corporate until there's enough corporate history to fit on (currently 533 training rows, tenure ≤ 6 months).
- **Don't deploy yet** — the honest AUC is 0.644 against a claimed 0.967, and the segment that's about to be a third of the book ranks below chance.
- **Deploy with a tripwire** — run it, but state the measured AUC below which it gets pulled, and how it will be measured after launch (H2 §6 step 8).

I'd hold off committing until the dollar figure exists — but decide the *shape* now, so the number doesn't write the verdict for you. And D4 is still open: calibration on Sep 2025 (needs a refit inside the A3 script), on the test window, or skipped?

Three separate things. Taking them in the order that matters least to most.

## D5 — not quite; the brief splits them

Re-reading A3: item 1 requires **AUC** overall, corporate, and everyone else, each with row and churner counts — that's mandatory, and the rubric repeats it ("Test AUC overall and by segment with row and churner counts"). Item 3 asks for "the realised dollar value of the program on the test period at that threshold, and compare it with offering to every member" — no segment requirement.

So segment dollars are *optional*. I still recommend them, because your recommendation's condition will almost certainly be about corporate, and "corporate loses $X" is a far stronger sentence than "corporate ranks at 0.404." But it's a choice, not an obligation, and it costs a couple of lines of your page budget.

## D6 — why the shape comes first

The exam-ready version is two sentences: **the evidence you already have tells you the direction; the dollar figure only tells you the magnitude.** And a recommendation that a single realised number could flip isn't a recommendation — it's a readout of that number.

Unpacking it, four reasons:

1. **The figure is one realisation, not the risk.** Same distinction we worked through in session 2: the realised value on 4,556 rows with 408 churners is an empirical quantity with sampling error, not the expected value of the programme. If its sign decides your verdict, your verdict inherits its noise.

2. **The direction is already determined by things the number can't change.** Honest AUC 0.644 against a claimed 0.967; corporate at 0.404, below chance; corporate going from 19% of the test book to a forecast 35%; the model's single largest term being the channel itself. Whether the quarter lands at +$8,000 or +$12,000, none of that moves.

3. **The condition has to come from a mechanism, not from a total.** "Deploy while corporate stays under X% of the book" is a claim about *why* the model earns money — it ranks non-corporate members and not corporate ones. You can only write that sentence from the structure. A dollar total can't tell you what to condition on.

4. **This is R1 §4.9's rule applied one level up.** "Choose the metric before fitting anything, and write it down. Choosing afterwards, from a table of several, is selection on the outcome — the same error as choosing a model on the test set."

And the reason that's worth doing rather than just principled: pre-committing is what makes you able to be **surprised**. Course doc §3 — "Write down what you expect before you compute anything... that is what makes you able to be surprised, and being surprised is how you find things." If you expect the corporate line to be negative and it comes out strongly positive, that's a finding worth chasing. If you had no expectation, it's just a number you write down. Deciding the shape first doesn't mean ignoring the number; it means the number can inform you instead of merely instructing you.

## D4 — what calibration is

First a correction: **p comes from the row-3 model**, not a fourth model. Row 4 fits nothing — it takes row 3's 4,556 scores and splits them by channel. There are only four fits in A2 (rows 0, 1, 2 inside folds; row 3 once on the training window).

Now the substance. You're right that p is unitless and that we *call* it a probability — the model outputs a number in (0,1) and we label it "probability this member-month ends in cancellation." Calibration is the question of whether that label is **true**: among all the rows where the model said p ≈ 0.25, did about 25% actually churn? (R1 §4.7: "A model is calibrated if, among accounts assigned p̂ ≈ 0.2, about 20% default.")

The reason this is a real question and not pedantry: **AUC cannot see it.** AUC is a statement about ordering only — R1 §4.5 spells it out, "a model whose probabilities are all ten times too large has exactly the same AUC." So our 0.644 tells us the model ranks tolerably and tells us *nothing* about whether p = 0.25 means 25%.

And it matters here specifically because of what we're about to do with p. The threshold derivation is 84p − 18 > 0, which treats p as a genuine probability. If the model is systematically over-confident — says 0.25 where the truth is 0.10 — then thresholding at 0.214 selects members whose real churn probability is well below 0.214, and every one of those offers loses money in expectation. The realised dollar figure would come out worse than the derivation promises, and without a calibration check we'd have no way to say why.

There's a concrete reason to suspect drift: the model was fitted where the base rate is **8.40%** (1,767 of 21,031) and applied where it is **8.96%** (408 of 4,556) — close, so probably fine here — but deployment is Apr–Jun, which ran 5.3–7.1% in 2025. A model carrying an 8.4% prior into a 6% book will read high across the board, which pushes more members over a fixed threshold than should be.

So the options again, now that the stakes are clear: check on Sep 2025 (clean, untouched, but the A3 script must refit the identical model since `a2_evaluation.py` is staying closed); check on the test window as reporting only; or skip and state the assumption in the memo. Given your two days and that the test window's base rate is close to training, skipping is defensible — but then the memo should say plainly that the threshold assumes calibrated probabilities and that this was not verified.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-29T23:42:28.087Z]
==============================================================================

Two different operations get called "calibration," and the window answer differs for each. Worth separating before you choose.

## Checking versus correcting

**Checking** is a diagnostic. Sort the scored rows, bin them (say ten equal-count bins), and in each bin compare the mean predicted p against the observed churn rate. If the model is calibrated the pairs sit on the diagonal — that's R1 Figure 5, the reliability plot. Nothing changes; you learn whether p means what it claims.

**Correcting** — recalibration proper — fits a function that maps raw p to a corrected p, then uses the corrected values. The two standard ones are Platt scaling (a one-variable logistic regression of the outcome on the raw score) and isotonic regression (a monotone step function). Both change which members clear the threshold, and therefore change the dollar figure.

## Which window

- **Checking**: either. Validation is the natural home; the test window is defensible as reporting-only since it's already been scored.
- **Correcting**: **validation only, never test.** A correction is a function fitted to data — it has parameters, it's chosen by looking at outcomes. Fitting it on the test window would be selection on the test set, which is the one thing the brief forbids outright. This is exactly the role the brief assigns Sep 2025: "any choice made by looking at data."

So the legitimate sequence is: fit the correction on Sep 2025, apply it to the Oct–Dec scores, price the programme at t\* on the corrected scores.

One helpful fact if you do correct: both Platt and isotonic are monotone, so they **cannot change the AUC**. Your A2 table stays valid as written — recalibration moves the numbers p, not their order.

## What I'd recommend, and the reason to be careful

**Check on Sep 2025, report it, and correct only if the check shows a material problem.** Two reasons for the caution:

- Sep 2025 has 1,836 rows and 198 churners — a **10.8% base rate**, higher than both the training window (8.4%) and the test window (9.0%). A correction fitted there would push probabilities *upward*, which is the wrong direction for a test window that is closer to the training base rate, and the wrong direction again for an Apr–Jun deployment running 5.3–7.1%. You could easily make the dollar figure worse by correcting toward a month that isn't representative of either.
- One month is thin for fitting a correction, and September sits right after the competitor-launch spike.

So the honest version is: check it, and if the check comes back reasonable, state in the memo that the threshold assumes calibrated probabilities and that this was verified on the validation month. If it comes back badly off, that's a finding — and then correcting becomes a decision you make deliberately, with the before-and-after both reported.

**Mechanically**, since `a2_evaluation.py` stays closed: the A3 script imports `fit_once` and `ADMISSIBLE` from it, refits on the same training window, and scores Sep 2025. Same code path, no duplication, A2 untouched, and the test window is still only ever scored by row 3.

That leaves **D6** — the shape of the recommendation. Once you give me that, I'll write the A3 script.

==============================================================================

"Shape" means the logical form of the sentence the VP reads — what you're advising and what, if anything, it hangs on. Here are the candidates as actual sentences, with a placeholder where the number goes.

**1. Unconditional go.**
> "Run the programme. On Oct–Dec 2025 it would have returned $X against $Y for offering to everyone."

Checkable: nothing to check — that's the point, and the weakness. Requires you to believe the test quarter carries forward to Apr–Jun. Hard to defend given corporate goes 19% → 35% and the model ranks corporate at 0.404.

**2. Unconditional stop.**
> "Do not run the programme. The honest ranking is 0.644, not 0.967, and on the segment forecast to be a third of the April book the model ranks below chance."

Checkable: nothing — again the point. Defensible if the dollar figure is negative or trivial. Weak if it's solidly positive, because you'd be leaving money on the table over a segment that is currently only 19% of the book.

**3. Conditional on composition.**
> "Run it while corporate members stay below N% of the active book; re-check that share at the start of each month, and suspend the programme if it exceeds N."

Checkable: Membership Analytics already reports channel mix monthly. Needs you to pick N and justify it — which means computing where the blended value crosses zero as the corporate share rises. That's a small extra calculation on the test scores, no new scoring.

**4. Scoped to a segment.**
> "Run it on non-corporate members only. Exclude corporate until there is enough corporate history to fit on — currently 533 training rows, none with more than six months of tenure."

Checkable: the exclusion is a rule someone implements; the re-entry condition is a row count or a tenure threshold you state. Directly matches the evidence, and it's the shape the segment dollar line will most likely support.

**5. Deploy with a tripwire.**
> "Run it, and measure AUC on the realised 60-day outcomes each quarter. Pull it if segment AUC falls below 0.55, or if the realised value over a quarter is negative."

Checkable: explicitly. This is Handout 2 §6 step 8 — "after launch, monitor the same metric on live data." Weakness on its own: it defers the judgment rather than making it, so it works best bolted onto 3 or 4 rather than alone.

They combine. The most common defensible answer here is **4 + 5**: scope it to where the evidence supports it, and state the condition under which the excluded segment comes back in. But 3 + 5 is just as defensible if you'd rather keep the programme whole and govern it by mix.

What makes this a judgment and not a calculation: the evidence constrains you to something conditional, but *which* condition — a share of the book, a segment exclusion, a measured-AUC tripwire — depends on what you think Northline can actually operate and check. That's the part the numbers can't settle.

I like the combination of 4 and 5 where we are being honest about the capabilities of the model since there is a lack of historical data for the corporate segment, and we are also still keeping the main goal in mind which is to create value throguh the programme, so if the realized 6 0day outcomes each quarter are negative or the AUC approaches a random coin flip a little too much, running the model at that point is probably just a compute cost and not really makking or saving us any money.

(4), t* = C_FP / (C_FP + C_FN), with a\n    # wasted offer as the false positive and a missed churner as the false\n    # negative.  Two routes agreeing is the check.\n    t_r1 = cost_stayer / (cost_stayer + gain_churner)\n    assert abs(t - t_r1) < 1e-12, (t, t_r1)\n\n    lines = [\n        \"Deriving the threshold from the finance table\",\n        \"\",\n        f\"  offer cost                     ${OFFER_COST:,.2f}\",\n        f\"  lifetime value of a member     ${MEMBER_LTV:,.2f}\",\n        f\"  fraction of churners retained  {OFFER_CONVERSION}\",\n        \"\",\n        \"An offer to a member who would have churned is worth\",\n        f\"  {MEMBER_LTV:,.0f} x {OFFER_CONVERSION} - {OFFER_COST:,.0f} = ${gain_churner:,.2f}\",\n        \"An offer to a member who would not have churned costs\",\n        f\"  ${cost_stayer:,.2f}\",\n        \"No offer is worth $0.\",\n        \"\",\n        \"At predicted probability p the expected value of offering is\",\n        f\"  E[value | p] = p x {gain_churner:,.0f} + (1 - p) x (-{cost_stayer:,.0f})\"\n        f\" = {gain_churner + cost_stayer:,.0f}p - {cost_stayer:,.0f}\",\n        \"\",\n        \"Offering beats not offering when that expectation exceeds 0:\",\n        f\"  {gain_churner + cost_stayer:,.0f}p - {cost_stayer:,.0f} > 0\",\n        f\"  p > {cost_stayer:,.0f} / {gain_churner + cost_stayer:,.0f}\"\n        f\" = {OFFER_COST:,.0f} / ({MEMBER_LTV:,.0f} x {OFFER_CONVERSION})\",\n        f\"  p > {t:.6f}\",\n        \"\",\n        f\"Threshold t* = {t:.4f}.  Offer to every member scored above it.\",\n        \"\",\n        \"Cross-check, Handout R1 eq. (4): t* = C_FP / (C_FP + C_FN) with a\",\n        f\"wasted offer C_FP = ${cost_stayer:,.0f} and a missed churner\",\n        f\"C_FN = ${gain_churner:,.0f} gives {cost_stayer:,.0f}/\"\n        f\"({cost_stayer:,.0f}+{gain_churner:,.0f}) = {t_r1:.6f}.  Same number.\",\n        \"\",\n        \"Note this depends only on the finance figures, not on the data, and\",\n        \"was fixed before any test-window score was read.  The analyst's\",\n        f\"script instead grid-searched {ANALYST_THRESHOLD} by maximising realised value\",\n        \"on its own out-of-fold predictions, which is a choice made by looking\",\n        \"at outcomes.\",\n    ]\n    return t, lines\n\n\ndef logit(p: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n    p = np.clip(p, 1e-12, 1 - 1e-12)\n    return np.log(p / (1 - p))\n\n\ndef reliability(y: np.ndarray, p: np.ndarray, bins: int) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"Equal-count bins: mean predicted probability against observed rate.\"\"\"\n    order = np.argsort(p)\n    groups = np.array_split(order, bins)\n    rows = []\n    for i, idx in enumerate(groups, start=1):\n        rows.append({\n            \"bin\": i,\n            \"rows\": len(idx),\n            \"mean_predicted\": round(float(p[idx].mean()), 4),\n            \"observed_rate\": round(float(y[idx].mean()), 4),\n            \"churners\": int(y[idx].sum()),\n        })\n    out = pd.DataFrame(rows)\n    out[\"predicted_minus_observed\"] = (out[\"mean_predicted\"] - out[\"observed_rate\"]).round(4)\n    return out\n\n\ndef calibration_check(model) -> tuple[pd.DataFrame, LogisticRegression, list[str]]:\n    \"\"\"Score the validation month and ask whether p means what it claims.\"\"\"\n    df, _ = load_clean()\n    val = df[df[\"month\"] == VALID_MONTH].reset_index(drop=True)\n    y = val[TARGET].to_numpy()\n    p = model.predict_proba(val[ADMISSIBLE])[:, 1]\n\n    table = reliability(y, p, N_CALIBRATION_BINS)\n\n    # Platt scaling: a logistic regression of the outcome on the log-odds of\n    # the model's own score.  If the model were already calibrated the fit\n    # would be slope 1, intercept 0, so the coefficients read directly as\n    # \"how far off, and in which direction\".\n    platt = Two things, one of which is a correction to what I told you earlier.

**Validation evidence (the basis for the decision):**

- Platt slope **0.788**, intercept **−0.219**. If the only problem were September being a high-churn month, the fix would be a level shift and the slope would come out near 1.0. It doesn't — the model's log-odds are too spread out, which is a property of the model, not of the month.
- Under-prediction in **8 of the 10 bins**, including the top bin where our threshold lives: predicted 0.265 against 0.290 observed.
- Validation AUC 0.695, so the ranking is fine. This is purely about the numbers attached to the ranking — exactly the split R1 §4.5 warns about.

**My earlier caution was wrong.** I said fitting on September (10.8% base rate) would over-correct upward for a test window nearer the training rate. The raw model's mean prediction on the test window is 0.0738, i.e. it under-predicts there too, because the test book is more corporate and the model assigns corporate low risk. So the correction moves in the right direction, not the wrong one.

**And a disclosure about how I found that out.** To check the direction I compared mean predicted against observed *on the test window*, and it came out raw 0.0738 versus corrected 0.0926 against an observed 0.0896. That is test-set information, and it must not be the reason we correct — using it that way would be selecting on the test set. The decision has to rest on the validation evidence above, which independently says correct: slope 0.788, systematic under-prediction through the decision region. The test comparison can be *reported* afterwards as a check that the correction did what validation predicted, the same way the test AUC is reported. I'd rather tell you I computed it than quietly not mention it.

**Mechanical effect:** 203 rows clear the threshold on raw scores, 242 on corrected. Both Platt and isotonic are monotone, so **the A2 AUCs are unchanged** — 0.644, 0.404, 0.641 all still describe the model producing the dollars.

So: set `CALIBRATION = "platt"`, on the validation evidence, and report the raw figure as a sensitivity line? Or stay raw and state the miscalibration as a caveat? I'd correct — the threshold derivation treats p as a probability and the validation month says it isn't one.

A "sensitivity" is one extra line showing what the answer would have been under a different assumption, so the reader can see how much the conclusion depends on that assumption rather than having to take it on faith. It's not a second answer — it's a measure of how load-bearing a choice was.

You've already agreed to one: the repeat-contact line. The headline counts 203 offers; the sensitivity says "37 of those are repeat contacts; at most one contact per member it's 166 offers worth $Z." If $Z is close to the headline, the choice didn't matter much and nobody needs to argue about it. If it's wildly different, the VP has learned that the programme's value hinges on re-contacting people.

The calibration one would be the same move: headline on corrected probabilities, one line saying "on uncorrected probabilities the figure would have been $W." Its job is to show whether correcting was a detail or the whole ballgame — and it pre-empts the obvious challenge, since a reader who distrusts the correction can see immediately whether it changes the recommendation.

Two reasons it's worth the line here, and one against. For: the correction is the one place in A3 where we fitted something to data, so it's the most attackable step; and it costs a single sentence. Against: your §1 budget is already 1,051 words of prose across A1 and A2 before A3 exists.

Given it's a sentence and it defends the step most likely to be questioned, I'd keep it. Setting `CALIBRATION = "platt"` and running.

Base directory for this skill: /private/tmp/claude-501/bundled-skills/2.1.278/1a9b1ae4fe9c532b82209648b14295e1/dataviz

# Data Visualization

A chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns "make it look
good" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather
than by taste.

**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form
heuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design
system supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a
diverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);
the method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the
reference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your
brand, read that file's structure and substitute its values - touch nothing else.

> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**
> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe - run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.

## The procedure - do these in order

Color comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.

1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job - magnitude, identity, polarity, a
   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes
   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). -> `references/choosing-a-form.md`
2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential
   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) - each has one rule.
   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. -> `references/color-formula.md`
3. **VALIDATE the palette - run the script, don't reason about Delta E.**
   `node scripts/validate_palette.js "<hex,hex,...>" --mode light` (relative to
   this skill's base directory - or load it as `<script type="module">` in the
   chart's own page, where it reads
   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns
   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,
   the normal-vision floor, and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for
   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.
4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to
   the baseline, 2px lines, >=8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked
   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,
   selective direct labels. -> `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`
5. **Add the hover layer - by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship
   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.
   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger
   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. -> `references/interaction.md`
6. **Final accessibility pass.** For >= 2 series a legend is always present and <= 4
   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box - the title names
   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** - its own
   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic
   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.
7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout - open or
   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow
   before calling it done.

Then check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** - it is the catalog
of what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.

## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)

- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a
  generated hue - it folds into "Other," small multiples, or composite encoding.
- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different
  scale -> two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the
  #1 chart mistake - see anti-patterns.)*
- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series
  count must not repaint the survivors.
- **Sequential = one hue, light->dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray
  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.
- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD Delta E >= 8 is the
  target (OKLab ×100); 6-8 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A
  normal-vision floor below 15 is a hard FAIL - full-color readers can't tell the
  pair apart; re-step it on the adjacent pairlist (secondary encoding does not excuse
  this one); under `--pairs all` cut series or facet instead - see check 4. A contrast WARN
  obligates visible labels or a table view - it is not dismissable.
- **Thin marks; a legend always present for >= 2 series (none for one), with
  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**
- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** - values, labels, and legends
  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.
- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused
  for "series 4"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.

## Plugging in a design system

The method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference
instance - every value filled in - is `references/palette.md`.

| Parameter | What the system provides |
|---|---|
| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |
| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |
| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |
| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |
| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical - steps distinct from categorical |
| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |
| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |
| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |

To onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let
it snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.

## Reference files

| File | What it answers |
|------|-----------------|
| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |
| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |
| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |
| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |
| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of - build each in plain HTML |
| `references/anti-patterns.md` | **What goes wrong - check every chart against this** |
| `references/palette.md` | **The reference palette instance** - every parameter, filled in; swap for your brand's |
| `scripts/validate_palette.js` | Runnable six-checks validator (run it; don't eyeball) |

Full run is clean — 11.6 seconds, A3 included. Here are the numbers.

## Exhibit A3 — realised value, test quarter (Oct–Dec 2025)

| Policy | Offers | To churners | Wasted | Value |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| **Targeted at t\* = 0.2143** | 242 | 62 | 180 | **+$852** |
| Offer to every member-month | 4,556 | 408 | 4,148 | **−$47,736** |
| No programme | 0 | — | — | $0 |
| At the analyst's 0.267 | 108 | 33 | 75 | +$828 |

Threshold derivation: an offer to a churner is worth 240 × 0.35 − 18 = $66, to a stayer −$18, so E[value | p] = 84p − 18, positive when **p > 18/84 = 0.2143**. Cross-checks against R1 eq. (4), 18/(18+66), to the same number — both routes are computed from the starter constants in `a3_threshold.txt`.

By segment: **corporate −$78** (9 offers, 1 churner), **everyone else +$930** (233 offers, 61 churners).

## The thing I want to flag: $852 is a very small number

Not too good — too *small*, and that's the finding. Against the analyst's claimed $19,774/quarter it's a **23× overstatement**, but in absolute terms the honest programme earns about **$3.52 per offer on $18 spent**, roughly a 20% return on offer spend, and **~$3,400/year** on a book of 1,669 members — for a programme that requires monthly scoring and 242 personal check-ins a quarter.

The arithmetic behind why is worth having in the memo, because it's the real business constraint: the offer economics demand **precision above 21.4%** to break even, and the base rate is **9%**. You need a 2.4× lift just to stop losing money. The model achieves 25.6% precision in the top 5.3% of the book — barely clear. Corporate achieves **11.1%**, well below break-even, which is why its line is negative.

Two things that make $852 likely optimistic for the actual deployment window: April–June ran 5.3–7.1% churn against the test quarter's 9.0%, so fewer members clear a fixed threshold and there are fewer churners to find; and the book is forecast to go from 19% corporate to 35%, shifting weight onto the segment that loses money.

## Secondary findings, two of which change what the memo should say

- **The threshold was not the analyst's main error.** Their grid-searched 0.267 yields $828 against our derived $852 — a $24 difference. Deriving the threshold is right and the brief requires it, but the memo shouldn't imply it's where the money is. The gap from $19,774 to $852 is the leaked columns and the evaluation design, not the cut point.
- **Calibration cost us $30.** Raw scores would have given $882; corrected give $852. Worth noting precisely because it demonstrates the discipline — we chose the correction on validation evidence before computing any dollars, and it made the headline slightly worse. Had we chosen on dollars we'd have kept the raw scores.
- **Not selecting on the test set cost $264.** The curve peaks at $1,098 at threshold 0.236; we took $852 at the derived 0.214. Honest to report, and it's what the right-hand panel of the figure shows.
- Repeat contacts: 47 of the 242 offers, worth $78 between them; one-contact-per-member gives 195 offers worth $774.

## Where this leaves your recommendation

Shape 4 + 5 still fits, and the numbers sharpen it: excluding corporate moves the quarter from $852 to $930, and corporate's 11.1% precision against a 21.4% break-even is the mechanism to cite. But the tripwire now has a harder job than "is the AUC holding up" — at $3.52 per offer the programme is close enough to break-even that a modest drop in precision, or the April base rate being lower than October's, would put it underwater. Whether that's worth running at all is your call, and it's a legitimate fourth option alongside the three we discussed.

Want me to draft A3 now, or bank this and move to Part B given the deadline?

Yes — and it's sharper than hypothetical. Two of our three test months are already at or below break-even.

| Month | Base rate | Offers | Precision | Value | Per offer |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Oct 2025 | 9.5% | 114 | 21.9% | +$48 | $0.42 |
| Nov 2025 | 8.9% | 81 | 33.3% | +$810 | $10.00 |
| Dec 2025 | 8.4% | 47 | 21.3% | −$6 | −$0.13 |
| **Quarter** | 9.0% | 242 | 25.6% | **+$852** | $3.52 |

The whole $852 is essentially November. But the right reading isn't "December is a bad month" — with 47 offers, December's precision carries a standard error of about 6 points, so it's noise. The right reading is that **the programme sits close enough to break-even that ordinary month-to-month variation straddles zero.**

## The mechanism is calibration, not the base rate

This matters for where you point the tripwire. Value per offer is exactly 84 × (precision − 0.2143). If the model were perfectly calibrated, a fixed threshold would be **self-regulating**: you'd only ever offer to members whose true churn probability exceeds 21.4%, so every offer is positive in expectation no matter what the base rate is — a lower-churn April would simply produce *fewer* offers, not *worse* ones. Total value would shrink; value per offer would not go negative.

The danger is the model being wrong about p at the top of the range. Inside the offered set it predicts **28.6%** against an observed **25.6%** — it over-states the risk of exactly the members it selects, even after the Platt correction. That's what eats the margin. And an April book running 5.3–7.1% against a model fitted at 8.4% would push that over-prediction further in the same direction.

So the tripwire in your shape-5 should watch **realised precision in the offered set** (equivalently, realised value per offer), not only AUC. AUC can hold perfectly steady while calibration drifts and the programme goes underwater — the ranking stays good, the cut point stops meaning what it meant.

## One number the memo needs, and it's uncomfortable

The 242 offers are 242 independent-ish draws of +$66 or −$18. That gives the quarter's total a standard error of roughly **$570**:

> $852 ± ~$570 — positive, but not strongly distinguishable from zero.

A single realisation this close to zero can't carry a confident "deploy." It can carry "deploy scoped and instrumented," which is the shape you chose.

## Two things this changes

**D2 should probably be revisited.** You chose quarter-only on the expectation that the monthly split was uninformative. It isn't — it's the clearest evidence that the value is unstable. One extra exhibit row, no extra prose, and it pre-empts a VP who asks "is this $852 a month I can count on?"

**The uncertainty figure needs to come from the run** if it goes in the memo. It's four lines in `a3_decision.py`. Want me to add both — the monthly split and the standard error — before I draft A3?

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-30T00:12:06.714Z]
==============================================================================

Two different things, and your instinct is right about one of them.

## The over-prediction is not about repeat contacts

It's what happens when you select the extreme tail of a noisy estimate. Within the 242 offered rows, split into quartiles by score:

| Quartile of offered set | Offers | Model says | Actually churned | Gap |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 (just above t\*) | 61 | 22.2% | 18.0% | +4.1 |
| 2 | 60 | 24.3% | 23.3% | +0.9 |
| 3 | 60 | 28.1% | 33.3% | −5.2 |
| 4 (highest scores) | 61 | **39.6%** | **27.9%** | **+11.8** |

The worst over-statement is at the very top — the model's most confident predictions are its least reliable. That's the winner's curse: a member reaches the top of the ranking partly through genuine risk and partly through estimation error in their score, and the error doesn't repeat. Nothing to do with how often you contact them; it would happen on a single month's scoring of people you'd never contacted before.

With ~15 churners per quartile only the Q4 gap is much beyond noise (roughly 2 standard errors), so treat the row-by-row pattern as suggestive. The robust claim is the top-end one.

## But your instinct is separately correct

Repeat contacts *are* thinner:

| | Offers | Precision | Per offer |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| First contact | 195 | 26.2% | **$3.97** |
| Repeat contacts | 47 | 23.4% | **$1.66** |

Both still clear the 21.4% break-even, so repeats aren't loss-making in aggregate — but they earn less than half as much per dollar spent. And the concrete version: **43 members were flagged more than once; 32 of them never churned in the window, and the programme spent $1,224 chasing them.**

So re-contacting is a real drag, just a second-order one next to the top-end over-confidence.

## What this settles

Your tripwire call is the right one and now has a specific form: **realised value per offer**, measured on matured 60-day outcomes, with $0 as the trip point — which is the same thing as realised precision falling below 21.4%. That single number catches both failure modes at once: it moves if the ranking degrades, and it moves if calibration drifts while the ranking holds.

Given the clock, I'd stop investigating here. Everything above already supports your recommendation shape, and none of it is required by the rubric. Shall I add the three things that *are* memo-bound to the script — monthly split, the ±$570 standard error, and first-versus-repeat per-offer value — then draft A3?

Reported because the\n    total sits close enough to zero that its uncertainty changes how it reads.\n    \"\"\"\n    v = per_offer_values(y, offered)\n    return float(v.sum()), float(v.std(ddof=1) * np.sqrt(len(v)))\n\n\ndef value_by_month(scores: pd.DataFrame, y: np.ndarray, offered: np.ndarray,\n                   t_star: float) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"The same fixed threshold, month by month.\n\n    Value per offer is exactly 84 x (precision - t*), so a month's programme\n    is profitable precisely when the share of its offers that land on a\n    churner exceeds the break-even precision, which is t* itself.\n    \"\"\"\n    rows = []\n    for month, idx in scores.groupby(\"month\").groups.items():\n        m = np.zeros(len(scores), dtype=bool)\n        m[scores.index.get_indexer(idx)] = True\n        o = offered & m\n        n = int(o.sum())\n        k = int((o & (y == 1)).sum())\n        total, se = value_with_se(y, o) if n else (0.0, 0.0)\n        rows.append({\n            \"month\": month,\n            \"rows\": int(m.sum()),\n            \"base_rate\": round(float(y[m].mean()), 4),\n            \"offers\": n,\n            \"offers_to_churners\": k,\n            \"precision\": round(k / n, 4) if n else np.nan,\n            \"break_even_precision\": round(t_star, 4),\n            \"value_usd\": round(total, 2),\n            \"value_se_usd\": round(se, 2),\n            \"value_per_offer_usd\": round(total / n, 2) if n else np.nan,\n        })\n    return pd.DataFrame(rows)\n\n\ndef offer_quality(scores: pd.DataFrame, y: np.ndarray, offered: np.ndarray,\n                  t_star: float) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"Where inside the offered set the value comes from, two cuts.\n\n    First-versus-repeat contact is a property of the policy: a member may\n    clear the threshold in more than one month. And 0.2143\nis also the **break-even precision**: value per offer is exactly\n84 × (precision − 0.2143), so the programme pays only while more than 21.4% of\nits offers reach a member who would have cancelled.\n\n## Exhibit A3c — Realised value on the test quarter\n\nSource: `analysis/outputs/a3_value.csv`. Oct – Dec 2025, 4,556 member-months,\n408 churners. One offer = one member-month.\n\n| Policy | Offers | Reached a churner | Wasted | Value |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| **Targeted at t\\* = 0.2143** | 242 | 62 | 180 | **+$852** |\n| — corporate | 9 | 1 | 8 | **−$78** |\n| — everyone else | 233 | 61 | 172 | **+$930** |\n| Offer to every member-month | 4,556 | 408 | 4,148 | **−$47,736** |\n| No programme | 0 | — | — | $0 |\n| *Memo: at the analyst's 0.267* | 108 | 33 | 75 | +$828 |\n\nThe quarter's total carries a standard error of **$572** — each offer returns\n+$66 or −$18, and the total is a sum of 242 such draws, so $852 sits 1.5\nstandard errors above zero.\n\n## Exhibit A3d — Where the value comes from, and where it does not\n\nSource: `analysis/outputs/a3_by_month.csv`, `a3_offer_quality.csv`.\n\n| Month | Offers | Precision | Value | Per offer |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Oct 2025 | 114 | 21.9% | +$48 | $0.42 |\n| Nov 2025 | 81 | 33.3% | +$810 | $10.00 |\n| Dec 2025 | 47 | 21.3% | −$6 | −$0.13 |\n\n| Inside the offered set | Offers | Model says | Actually churned | Per offer |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| First contact | 195 | 28.6% | 26.2% | $3.97 |\n| Repeat contact | 47 | 28.4% | 23.4% | $1.66 |\n| Q1, just above t\\* | 61 | 22.2% | 18.0% | −$2.85 |\n| Q4, highest scores | 61 | **39.6%** | **27.9%** | $5.41 |\n\n**Figure A3** (`a3_value_curve.png`): realised value across the whole\nthreshold range, with the decision region at its own scale.\n\n---\n\n## Prose (draft, ~430 words; the recommendation above is the opening paragraph)\n\n**What the programme is worth.** Using the protocol in Exhibit A2 — admissible\ncolumns only, fitted on Jan 2024 to Jun 2025, scored once on Oct to Dec 2025 —\nthe targeting rule returns $852 on the quarter. The proposal in front of the\ncommittee says $19,774. The gap is not the threshold: applying the analyst's\nown 0.267 to honest scores returns $828, within $24 of our derived figure. The\ngap is that their number was computed on predictions that had already seen the\ncancellation, and on a random shuffle of two years rather than a fit to the\npast scored on the future.\n\n**Why the margin is thin.** An offer costs $18 and a retained churner returns\n$66, so the programme pays only while more than 21.4% of offers reach someone\nwho would have cancelled. The base rate is 9%. That requires finding members at\nroughly two and a half times the average risk, and the model manages 25.6% in\nthe top 5% of the book — profitable, but by four percentage points. Month to\nmonth that margin straddles zero: November returned $810, October $48 and\nDecember −$6. With 47 to 114 offers in a month those differences are mostly\nnoise, and that is the point — the programme sits close enough to break-even\nthat ordinary variation puts individual months on either side.\n\n**Why corporate comes out.** Corporate members were ranked below a coin flip\n(AUC 0.404 on 34 churners) and their nine offers landed on a churner once.\nThe model's single largest term is the acquisition channel itself, about four\ntimes the churn odds for any non-corporate channel, learned from 533 corporate\nrows none older than six months. It has learned that corporate members rarely\ncancel, not which of them will. Excluding them adds $78 to the quarter and\nremoves a segment forecast to triple its share of the book.\n\n**What to watch.** The threshold assumes the predicted probabilities mean what\nthey say. They roughly do — checked on the validation month, and corrected\nthere before use — but the model still overstates the risk of the members it\npicks, claiming 39.6% for its top-scoring offers and delivering 27.9%. That is\nwhat selecting the top of a noisy score does, and it is why the tripwire\nshould be a dollar figure rather than a ranking statistic: AUC can hold steady\nwhile the probabilities drift and the cut point stops meaning what it meant.\nTwo further reasons to expect less than $852 next spring: April to June ran\n5.3–7.1% churn against this quarter's 9.0%, and 1,294 members in the history\nalready received a retention contact, so part of the outcome we are fitting to\nis itself post-treatment.\n\n---\n\n## Notes to self (not memo text)\n\n### Design decisions (mine, session 4)\n\n- One offer = one member-month, matching the monthly programme and the brief's\n  per-offer economics. Per-member accounting was rejected: a churner has only\n  one flagged row, so \"which month did the single offer happen in\" has no\n  honest answer.\n- Quarter as the reporting period (the test window is exactly one quarter).\n  Monthly split added later, after it turned out to be informative rather than\n  decorative — see below.\n- Priced against offer-everyone, no programme, and the analyst's threshold.\n  Declined to score the test window with the analyst's *model*: that would be\n  a second scoring, and A2 already set the norm against it.\n- Calibration checked on the validation month and corrected there. The verdict\n  was set from validation evidence (Platt slope 0.79, under-prediction in 8 of\n  10 bins) **before** any dollar figure existed. It cost $30 — raw scores give\n  $882 — which is the point: chosen on calibration grounds, not on payout.\n- Value curve reported. It re-reads the same scores at 400 cut points; no new\n  scoring, and t* was fixed in advance. The curve peaks at $1,098 at 0.236;\n  not selecting on the test set cost $264, stated in the memo rather than hidden.\n- Recommendation shape chosen before the dollar figure was computed.\n\n### Arguing against the draft\n\n- **$852 ± $572 is 1.5 SE from zero.** A \"deploy\" that rests on it is resting\n  on a coin that landed the right way. The scoped-and-instrumented shape is\n  what the uncertainty supports; an unconditional go is not.\n- **Corporate's −$78 is nine offers and one churner.** The dollar line is\n  nearly meaningless on its own. The argument for exclusion rests on 34\n  churners at AUC 0.404, 533 training rows, and the mechanism (the model learned\n  the channel, not the members) — not on the $78.\n- **The quartile table is suggestive, not conclusive.** ~15 churners per\n  quartile; only the Q4 gap (39.6% claimed, 27.9% delivered) is about two\n  standard errors. Do not present Q1–Q3 as an established pattern.\n- **Repeat contacts are a second-order drag, not a defect.** $1.66 versus\n  $3.97 per offer, both above break-even. Worth a sentence, not a policy fight.\n  32 members were flagged more than once and never churned, costing $1,224.\n- **Seasonality cuts both ways.** Oct–Dec is a *higher*-churn quarter than\n  Apr–Jun, so the $852 is probably optimistic for deployment — but 2024 shows\n  the same summer peak, so the level is seasonal rather than a trend, and the\n  April book is not simply \"worse\".\n- **The recommendation excludes 19% of the current book and a forecast 35%.**\n  That is a large operational carve-out to justify on 34 churners. The honest\n  framing is that we cannot yet tell whether the model helps on corporate, not\n  that we know it hurts.\n\n### What changed my mind this session\n\n- I expected the monthly split to be decorative and advised skipping it (D2).\n  It turned out to carry the clearest evidence that the value is unstable, so\n  it went in. Recorded because it is exactly the kind of pre-commitment that\n  should be revisited when the data disagree.\n- I predicted that fitting the calibration on September (10.8% base rate)\n  would over-correct for a lower-rate test window. Wrong: the raw model\n  under-predicts on the test window too, because the test book is more\n  corporate and the model scores corporate low.\n\n### Trimming candidates for the two-page limit (need ~450 words out)\n\n1. A1's loss sentence — A3's break-even paragraph says it better (~40 words).\n2. A1's corporate and seasonality figures → move to exhibit footnotes (~90).\n3. A2's \"reading the rows\" row-by-row commentary → compress rows 0 and 2,\n   which moved least (~120).\n4. A2's odds-ratio prose → Exhibit A2c already carries it (~60).\n5. A3's final paragraph's post-treatment clause → exhibit footnote (~40).\n\n### Numbers used and where they live\n\n| Number | File |\n|---|---|\n| AUC 0.644 / 0.404 / 0.641; 4,556 / 408; 860 / 34; 3,696 / 374 | `a2_table.csv` |\n| t* = 0.2143, the derivation, both routes | `a3_threshold.txt` |\n| $852 / −$78 / +$930 / −$47,736 / $0 / $828; offers and churner counts | `a3_value.csv` |\n| SE $572; 1.5 SE from zero | `run.log` (from `value_with_se`) |\n| Monthly: 114/81/47 offers, 21.9/33.3/21.3%, $48/$810/−$6 | `a3_by_month.csv` |\n| First vs repeat $3.97 / $1.66; Q1 −$2.85; Q4 39.6% vs 27.9% | `a3_offer_quality.csv` |\n| Validation: Platt slope 0.788, intercept −0.219, 8 of 10 bins under | `a3_calibration.csv`, `run.log` |\n| Curve peak $1,098 at 0.236; raw-score sensitivity $882 | `a3_value_curve.csv`, `run.log` |\n| 533 corporate training rows, tenure ≤ 6 | `a1_corporate_segment.csv` |\n| Channel odds ratios ~4× | `a2_odds_ratios.csv` |\n| Apr–Jun 5.3–7.1% vs Oct–Dec 8.4–9.5% | `a1_base_rate_by_month.csv` |\n| 1,294 non-churners with `winback_offer_sent` = 1 | `a1_column_timing.csv` |\n"

That was\n  wrong and it said so: the raw model under-predicts on the test window too,\n  because the test book is more corporate and the model scores corporate low.\n- I chose one offer = one member-month over per-member accounting, and fixed\n  the shape of the recommendation (scope to non-corporate, plus a tripwire)\n  before any dollar figure was computed.\n- I rejected scoring the test window with the analyst's *model* for a\n  like-for-like dollar comparison: it would be a second scoring of the test\n  set, and session 3 had already set the norm against that.\n- I specified the tripwire in dollars — realised value per offer — rather than\n  AUC, after seeing that the model's top-scoring offers claim 39.6% and\n  deliver 27.9%, so a ranking statistic can hold steady while the programme\n  goes underwater.\n\n**How numbers were checked:**\n- Cross-machine: re-ran the whole pipeline on the Mac against outputs\n  committed from Windows. `a2_table.csv` and all six A1 outputs were\n  byte-identical; `a2_test_scores.csv` differed by at most 4e-14 in `p_churn`,\n  and the same 203 rows cleared the threshold on both machines, so no dollar\n  figure depends on the hardware.\n- The threshold is derived two ways in code — 18/(240 x 0.35) and the general\n  form C_FP/(C_FP+C_FN) — with an assertion that they agree, and both read the\n  constants from `starter/load.py` rather than being typed in.\n- The vectorised value calculation is asserted row by row against the supplied\n  `offer_value()` helper, so the economics come from the starter module.\n- A2's partitions were verified from the data (months present in each, no\n  overlap, all 24 accounted for) rather than from reading the code.\n- The calibration decision was made on validation evidence *before* any dollar\n  figure was computed, and it cost $30 — raw scores return $882 against $852\n  corrected. Deciding afterwards would have selected the raw scores.\n- The figure's annotation disagreed with the exhibit ($834 against $852)\n  because the curve grid did not pass through the derived threshold. Caught by\n  reading the figure against the table; fixed by putting t* and 0 in the grid.\n\n**Would still not bet on:** the corporate dollar line (-$78 on nine offers and\none churner); the quartile pattern below the top quartile (~15 churners each);\nand that $852 transfers to April-June, which runs a lower base rate on a book\nforecast at 35% corporate.\nEOF\necho \"appended.
